\paragraph{\DIFadd{Domain Pre-Training and Multitask Learning}} \DIFadd{\modelname{DARWIN 1.5} used a multitask approach to fine-tune }\DIFaddend \modelname{Llama-7B} through a two-stage process \autocite{xie2025darwin}. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Initially trained on 332k scientific question-answer }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In the first step, the base model was fine-tuned on $332k$ scientific }\gls{qa} \DIFaddend pairs to establish foundational scientific reasoning\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Subsequently}\DIFaddend , the model \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{subsequently }\DIFdelend underwent multitask learning \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{to perform property prediction-related }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{on $22$ different }\DIFaddend regression and classification tasks \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{concurrently. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{based on experimental datasets. \modelname{DARWIN 1.5}'s core idea is the use of }\gls{lift} \DIFadd{(see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:model_adaptation}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) on diverse materials tasks to induce cross-task transfer during training. However, in some cases, the results revealed negative task-interaction, i.e., some tasks had diminished performance under multi-tasking.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Building on similar principles, \modelname{nach0} introduced }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\modelname{ChemLLM} followed a similar approach to \modelname{DARWIN 1.5}: template-based instruction tuning (ChemData) on $~7M$ }\gls{qa} \DIFadd{pairs. }\autocite{zhang2024chemllm}\DIFadd{.
}

\DIFadd{While the above examples focus on combining different in-domain tasks, \modelname{nach0} coupled natural language with chemical data }\autocite{livne2024nach0}\DIFadd{, based on }\DIFaddend a unified encoder-decoder transformer architecture\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{for cross-domain chemical tasks }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{livne2024nach0}%%%
\DIFdel{. Pre-trained }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. The model was pre-trained }\DIFaddend using \gls{ssl} on \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{both natural language and chemical data, \modelname{nach0} tackles diverse downstream applications including molecular structure generation, chemical property prediction, and reaction prediction. Notably, the authors found that combining chemistry-specific tasks outperformed models trained on distinct task groups, suggesting that chemical reasoning benefits from focused domain integration. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{In the materials domain, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{qu2023leveraging} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
developed a language-driven materials discovery framework that uses transformer-based embeddings (e.g., \modelname{MatBERT}}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{wan2024tokens}%%%
\DIFdel{) to represent and generate novel crystal structures. Candidates are first recalled via similarity in embedding space, then ranked using a multitask multi-gate }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{moe} %%%
\DIFdel{model that predicts the desired properties jointly. 
Their method successfully identifies novel high-performance materials (e.g., halide perovskites) and demonstrates that language representations encode latent knowledge for task-agnostic materials design}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{a combination of }\gls{smiles} \DIFadd{strings and natural language from scientific literature, and then instruction-tuned on chemistry and }\gls{nlp} \DIFadd{tasks. This allows \modelname{nach0} to translate between natural language and }\gls{smiles}\DIFadd{, in addition to tasks like }\gls{qa}\DIFadd{, information extraction, and molecule/reaction generation}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Domain-Specific Pre-Training Strategies}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{A second category of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{emphasizes deep domain knowledge through specialized pre-training. }\DIFdelend \modelname{LLaMat} employed \gls{peft} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{specifically }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to continue the pre-training }\DIFaddend on crystal structure data in \gls{cif} format, enabling the generation of thermodynamically stable structures \autocite{mishra2024foundational}.

\modelname{ChemDFM} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{scales }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{scaled }\DIFaddend this concept significantly, implementing domain pre-training on over 34 billion tokens from chemical textbooks and research articles \autocite{zhao2024chemdfm}. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Through }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{After that, through }\DIFaddend comprehensive instruction tuning, \modelname{ChemDFM} familiarizes itself with chemical notation and patterns, distinguishing it from more materials-focused approaches like \modelname{LLaMat} through its broader chemical knowledge base.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{ChemLLM} further refined this approach by introducing template-based instruction tuning (ChemData) to optimize property-guided molecular generation }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{zhang2024chemllm}%%%
\DIFdel{.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \paragraph{Reasoning-Based Approaches} A recent development in chemical \glspl{gpm} incorporates explicit reasoning capabilities. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{ether0} demonstrates }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The \modelname{ether0} model demonstrated }\DIFaddend this approach as a 24 \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{billion parameter }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{billion-parameter }\DIFaddend reasoning model trained on over 640k \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{experimentally-grounded chemistry problems across diverse }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{chemically-verifiable problems (e.g., through code) across 375 }\DIFaddend tasks, including \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{retrosynthesis, solubility }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{single-step retrosynthesis, molecular }\DIFaddend editing, and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{property }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reaction }\DIFaddend prediction \autocite{narayanan2025training}. 
Unlike previous models, \modelname{ether0}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{uses }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{'s training used a novel }\DIFaddend \gls{rl} \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{approach }\DIFaddend (see \Cref{sec:rl})\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{to develop reasoning behaviors like verification and backtracking, demonstrating }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. First, \modelname{DeepSeek-R1} was prompted to create long }\gls{cot} \DIFadd{traces ending in a }\gls{smiles}\DIFadd{. These traces were used to fine-tune a pre-trained \modelname{Mistral-Small-24B} using }\gls{sft}\DIFadd{, resulting in a }\enquote{base reasoner} \DIFadd{model. This model underwent }\gls{rl} \DIFadd{using }\gls{grpo} \DIFadd{on several verifiable chemical tasks to create }\enquote{specialist} \DIFadd{models---one for each task. High-quality outputs from these specialist models were selected and used to fine-tune the }\enquote{base reasoner} \DIFadd{to create a }\enquote{generalist} \DIFadd{model, capable of reasoning about all the tasks. In the last step, the generalist model undergoes another round of }\gls{rl} \DIFadd{on the chemical tasks to improve the performance. This method shows }\DIFaddend that structured problem-solving approaches can significantly improve performance on complex chemical tasks \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{while maintaining grounding in experimental data}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{without the need for massive domain-specific corpora}\DIFaddend . 

These diverse approaches illustrate the evolving landscape of chemical \glspl{gpm}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, each balancing broad applicability with domain-specific precision}\DIFdelend .
Still, most applications of \glspl{gpm} focus on using \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{these }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{such }\DIFaddend models for one specific application\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend and we will review those in the following.

\DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Limitations}}
\DIFadd{Chemical }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{are emerging, but we lack systematic understanding of how to build effective ones.
}

\DIFadd{The tokenization question remains unresolved. Domain-specific tokenizers might improve data efficiency, but they prevent reuse of models trained on general text datasets. The tradeoff remains unclear.
}

\DIFadd{A deeper challenge exists: we might lack sufficient high-quality data. Existing chemical datasets are much smaller than those used in domains like mathematics (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:data-section}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). The literature reports only successes. Failed experiments, discarded results, and negative outcomes never appear in published datasets. More critically, chemical practitioners rely on tacit knowledge}\autocite{Polanyi_2009}\DIFadd{---expertise acquired through experience that experts cannot fully articulate. This implicit understanding remains absent from existing datasets.
}









\DIFaddend \subsection{Knowledge Gathering}\label{sec:information_gathering}
The rate of publishing keeps growing, and as a result, it is increasingly challenging to manually collect all relevant knowledge, potentially stymying scientific progress.\autocite{schilling2025text, Chu_2021}
Even though knowledge collection might seem like a simple task, it often involves multiple \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{different }\DIFdelend steps, visually described in \Cref{fig:knowledge_gathering}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. We split the discussion in this section in two: }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A. 
Here, we focus on }\DIFaddend structured data extraction and question answering. Example queries for both sections are in \Cref{fig:knowledge_gathering}B.


\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[!ht]
%DIFDELCMD <     \centering
%DIFDELCMD < \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure12.pdf}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\textbf{\DIFdelFL{A. a representation of a typical agent for scientific queries.}} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{The }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdelFL{is the central piece of the system, surrounded by typical tools that improve its question-answering capabilities, together forming an agentic system. The tools represented in this figure are semantic search, citation traversal, evidence gathering, and question answering. Semantic search finds relevant documents. Evidence gathering ranks and filters chunks of text using }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm}%%%
\DIFdelFL{. The citation traversal tool provides model access to citation graphs, enabling accurate referencing of each chunk and facilitating the discovery of additional sources. Finally, the question-answering tool (an }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm}%%%
\DIFdelFL{) collects all the information found by other tools and generates a final response to a user's query. This part the figure is inspired by the \modelname{PaperQA2} agent.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{skarlinski2024language} %%%
\textbf{\DIFdelFL{B.}} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{two examples of applications discussed in this section.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:knowledge_gathering}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{figure}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \paragraph{Semantic Search} A step that is key to most, if not all, knowledge-gathering tasks is \gls{rag}, discussed in more detail in \Cref{sec:rag}. 
Most commonly, this involves semantic search, intended to identify chunks of text with similar meaning. 
The difference between semantic search and conventional search lies in how each approach interprets queries. The latter operates through lexical matching---whether exact or fuzzy---focusing on the literal words and their variations. Semantic search, however, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{goes deeper by interpreting }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{focuses on }\DIFaddend the underlying meaning and contextual relationships within the content. 

To enable semantic search, documents are \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{stored in vector databases using embedding vectors }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{converted into embeddings }\DIFaddend (see \Cref{sec:embeddings}) \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.}\DIFdelend \autocite{bojanowski2017enriching}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{They represent the content of a document as a vector in a learned vector space and hence }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. 
They }\DIFaddend allow for similarity search by vector comparison (e.g., using cosine similarity for small databases or more sophisticated algorithms like \gls{hnsw} for large databases\autocite{malkov2018efficient}). 

In chemistry, semantic search has been used extensively to classify and identify chemical text.\autocite{Guo2021,beltagy2019scibert0,trewartha2022quantifying}

\DIFaddbegin \begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
\includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/final_figures/chemrev_figure12.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{\DIFaddFL{A. A representation of a typical agent for scientific queries.}} \DIFaddFL{The }\gls{llm} \DIFaddFL{is the central piece of the system, surrounded by typical tools that improve its question-answering capabilities, together forming an agentic system. The tools represented in this figure are semantic search, citation traversal, evidence gathering, and question answering. Semantic search finds relevant documents. Evidence gathering ranks and filters chunks of text using }\glspl{llm}\DIFaddFL{. The citation traversal tool provides model access to citation graphs, enabling accurate referencing of each chunk and facilitating the discovery of additional sources. Finally, the question-answering tool (an }\gls{llm}\DIFaddFL{) collects all the information found by other tools and generates a final response to a user's query. This part of the figure is inspired by the \modelname{PaperQA2} agent.}\autocite{skarlinski2024language} \textbf{\DIFaddFL{B.}} \DIFaddFL{Two examples of applications are discussed in this section. }\textbf{\DIFaddFL{C.}} \DIFaddFL{worked example using the }\texttt{\DIFaddFL{FutureHouse}}\DIFaddFL{'s platform (the Crow agent). }\enquote{used in 1.1} \DIFaddFL{indicates that this reference has been used in the final response of the model}\autocite{skarlinski2025futurehouse}}
\label{fig:knowledge_gathering}
\end{figure}

\DIFaddend \subsubsection{Structured Data Extraction}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Semantic search can help us find relevant resources. 
However, for many applications}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For many applications, }\DIFaddend it can be useful to collect data in a structured form, e.g., tables with fixed columns.
Obtaining such a dataset \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{based on }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{by }\DIFaddend extracting data from the literature using \glspl{llm} is currently one of the most practical avenues for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the use of }\DIFdelend \glspl{llm} in the chemical sciences \autocite{schilling2025text}.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Currently, }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{are used in various forms for this application. 
}\DIFdelend 

\paragraph{Data Extraction Using Prompting} 

For most applications, zero-shot prompting should be the starting point. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In this context, zero-shot }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Zero-shot }\DIFaddend prompting has been used to extract data about organic reactions\autocite{rios2025llm,vangala2024suitability, Patiny2023automatic}, synthesis procedures for metal-organic frameworks\autocite{zheng2023chatgpt}, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{polymers}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{polymer data}\DIFaddend \autocite{schilling2024using,gupta2024data}, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{solar cells}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{shabih2025automated}%%%
\DIFdel{, or }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{or other }\DIFaddend materials data\autocite{polak2024extracting,hira2024reconstructing,kumar2025mechbert,wu2025large,huang2022batterybert}. 

\paragraph{Fine-tuning Based Data Extraction} 

If a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{commercial }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(commercial) }\DIFaddend model needs to be run very often, it can be more cost-efficient to fine-tune a smaller, open-source model compared to prompting a large model \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:distillation}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
)}\DIFaddend . 
In addition, models might lack specialized knowledge and might not follow certain style guides, which can be introduced with fine-tuning. 
\textcite{ai2024extracting} fine-tuned the \modelname{LLaMa-2-7B} model to extract \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{procedural }\DIFdelend chemical reaction data from \gls{uspto} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, and converted it to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{into }\DIFaddend a \gls{json} format compatible with the schema of \gls{ord}\autocite{Kearnes_2021}, achieving an overall accuracy of more than $90\%$. 
In a different \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{work}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{approach}\DIFaddend , \textcite{zhang2024fine} fine-tuned \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{a closed-source model --- }\DIFaddend \modelname{GPT-3.5-Turbo} to recognize and extract chemical entities from \gls{uspto}. Fine-tuning improved the performance of the base model on the same task by more than $15\%$. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Similarly, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{dagdelen2024structured} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
proposed }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{dagdelen2024structured} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
went beyond the afore-mentioned methods by using }\DIFaddend a human-in-the-loop data annotation process\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, where humans correct }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Here, humans corrected }\DIFaddend the outputs from an \gls{llm} extraction instead of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{extracting }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{annotating }\DIFaddend data from scratch.

\paragraph{Agents for Data Extraction} 

Agents (\Cref{sec:agents}) have shown their potential in data extraction, though to a limited extent.\autocite{chen2024autonomous,kang2024chatmof}
For example, \textcite{ansari2024agent} introduced \modelname{Eunomia}, an agent that autonomously extracts structured materials \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{science }\DIFdelend data from scientific literature without requiring fine-tuning\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, demonstrating performance comparable to or better than fine-tuned methods}\DIFdelend . Their agent is an \gls{llm} with access to tools such as chemical databases (e.g., the \modelname{Materials Project} database) and research papers from various sources. \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The document search tool leverages an }\gls{api}\DIFadd{-based embedding model to find the top-k most relevant passages based on semantic similarity. Other tools used include the chain-of-verification tool, which queries the agent independently (to avoid bias) in order to generate verification queries and finally produces a verified response.
}\DIFaddend 

While the authors claim this approach simplifies dataset creation for materials discovery, the evaluation is limited to a narrow set of materials science tasks (mostly focusing on \glspl{mof}), indicating the need for the creation of agent evaluation tools.


\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Limitations}} 
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The ability to extract data from sources other than text is important since a large amount of data is only stored in plots, tables, and figures. 
Despite some initial simple proofs of concept }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{Zheng2024image}%%%
\DIFdel{, the main bottleneck presently is the limited understanding of image data compared to text data in multimodal models.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{alampara2024probing} %%%
\DIFdel{The promise of agents lies in their ability to interact with tools (that can also interpret multimodal data). Moreover, their ability to self-reflect could automatically improve wrong results.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{du2023improving}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{Question Answering}
Besides extracting information from documents in a structured format, \glspl{llm} can also be used to answer questions---such as \enquote{Has X been tried before} by synthesizing knowledge from a corpus of documents (and potentially automatically retrieving additional documents). 

An example of a system that can do that is \modelname{PaperQA}. This agentic system contains tools for search, evidence-gathering, and question answering as well as for traversing citation graphs, which are shown in \Cref{fig:knowledge_gathering}. The evidence-gathering tool collects the most relevant chunks of information via the semantic search and performs \gls{llm}-based re-ranking of these chunks (i.e.\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend the \gls{llm} changes the order of the chunks depending on what is needed to answer the query).
Subsequently, only the top-$n$ most relevant chunks are kept. To further ground the responses, citation traversal tools (e.g., Semantic Scholar\autocite{kinney2023semantic}) are used. 
These leverage the citation graph as a means of discovering supplementary literature references. Ultimately, to address the user's query, a question-answering tool \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(a specially prompted }\gls{llm}\DIFadd{) }\DIFaddend is employed. It initially augments the query with all the collected information before providing a definitive answer.
The knowledge aggregated by these systems could be used to generate new hypotheses or challenge existing ones. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Thus, in the next section, we focus on this aspect}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Limitations}} 

\Glspl{llm} \DIFadd{and }\gls{llm}\DIFadd{-based systems are valuable information-gathering tools, but they have critical limitations. Their knowledge becomes outdated immediately after training. Without additional tools, they cannot identify when retrieved sources have been retracted or corrected. 
}

\DIFadd{Effective knowledge gathering requires human-}\gls{ai} \DIFadd{collaboration. Current systems struggle to ask relevant clarifying questions.}\autocite{choudhury2025bed0llm0} \DIFadd{Domain-specific benchmarks for evaluating this capability remain absent, hindering progress in developing truly interactive knowledge-gathering agents}\DIFaddend .

\DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}

\DIFadd{Critical challenges remain unresolved:
}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Multimodal Understanding.}} \DIFadd{Current models cannot reliably extract data from plots, tables, and figures.}\autocite{alampara2024probing,gupta2022discomat0} \DIFadd{This severely limits data extraction because substantial chemical knowledge exists only in visual formats. Improved multimodal capabilities are essential for comprehensive literature mining.
    }

    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Source Quality Assessment.}} \DIFadd{Citation counts and journal impact factors provide crude quality signals. How to select high-quality sources. Scholarly metrics alone are insufficient.
}\end{itemize}


\DIFaddend \subsection{Hypothesis Generation} \label{sec:hypothesis-gen}

Coming up with new hypotheses represents a cornerstone of the scientific process \autocite{rock2018hypothesis}. Historically, hypotheses have emerged from systematic observation of natural phenomena, exemplified by Isaac Newton’s formulation of the law of universal gravitation \autocite{newton1999principia}, which was inspired by the seemingly mundane observation of a falling apple \autocite{kosso2017whatgoesup}.

In modern research, hypothesis generation increasingly relies on data-driven tools. 
For example, clinical research employs frameworks such as \gls{viads} to derive testable hypotheses from well-curated datasets \autocite{Jing2022roles}. Similarly, advances in \glspl{llm} are now being explored for their potential to automate and enhance idea generation across scientific domains \autocite{oneill2025sparks}. However, such approaches face significant challenges due to the inherently open-ended nature of scientific discovery \autocite{stanley2017openendedness}. 
Open-ended domains, as discussed in \Cref{sec:data-section}, risk intractability, as an unbounded combinatorial space of potential variables, interactions, and experimental parameters complicates systematic exploration \autocite{clune2019ai0gas0}.
Moreover, the quantitative evaluation of the novelty and impact of generated hypotheses remains non-trivial. 
As Karl Popper argued, scientific discovery defies rigid logical frameworks \autocite{popper1959logic}, and objective metrics for \enquote{greatness} of ideas are elusive \autocite{stanley2015greatness}. These challenges underscore the complexity of automating or systematizing the creative core of scientific inquiry.

\subsubsection{Initial Sparks}

Recent efforts in the \gls{ml} community have sought to simulate the hypothesis formulation process \autocite{Gu2025forecasting, arlt2024meta0designing}, primarily leveraging multi-agent systems \autocite{jansen2025codescientist0, kumbhar2025hypothesis}. 
In such frameworks, agents typically retrieve prior knowledge to contextualize previous related \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{work---grounding }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{work, grounding }\DIFaddend hypothesis generation in existing literature \autocite{naumov2025dora, ghareeb2025robin0, gu2024interesting}. 
A key challenge, however, lies in evaluating the generated hypotheses. 
While some studies leverage \glspl{llm} to evaluate novelty or interestingness \autocite{zhang2024omni0}, recent work has introduced critic agents---specialized components designed to monitor and iteratively correct outputs from other agents---into multi-agent frameworks (see \Cref{sec:multi-agent}). 
For instance, \textcite{Ghafarollahi2024} demonstrated how integrating such critics enables systematic hypothesis refinement through continuous feedback mechanisms.

However, the reliability of purely model-based evaluation remains contentious. 
\textcite{si2025llms} argued that relying on a \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{llm} \DIFaddend to evaluate hypotheses lacks robustness, advocating instead for human assessment. 
This approach was adopted in their work, where human evaluators validated hypotheses produced by their system, finding more novel \gls{llm}-produced hypotheses compared to the ones proposed by humans.
Notably, \textcite{yamada2025ai} advanced the scope of such systems by automating the entire research \gls{ml} process, from hypothesis generation to article writing. 
Their system’s outputs were submitted to workshops at the \gls{iclr} 2025, with one contribution ultimately accepted. However, the advancements made by such works are currently incremental instead of unveiling new, paradigm-shifting research (see \Cref{fig:hypothesis-generation}).

\subsubsection{Chemistry-Focused Hypotheses}

In \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{scientific fields such as }\DIFdelend chemistry and materials science, hypothesis generation requires domain intuition, mastery of specialized terminology, and the ability to reason through foundational concepts \autocite{miret2024llms}. 
To address potential knowledge gaps in \glspl{llm}, \textcite{wang2023scimon0} proposed a few-shot learning approach (see \Cref{sec:prompting}) for hypothesis generation and compared it with model fine-tuning for the same task. 
Their method strategically incorporates in-context examples to supplement domain knowledge while discouraging over-reliance on existing literature. 
For fine-tuning, they designed a loss function that penalizes possible biases---e.g., given the context \enquote{hierarchical tables challenge numerical reasoning}, the model would be penalized if it generated an overly generic prediction like \enquote{table analysis} instead of a task-specific one---when trained on such examples. 
Human evaluations of ablation studies revealed that \modelname{GPT-4}, augmented with a knowledge graph of prior research, outperformed fine-tuned models in generating hypotheses with greater technical specificity and iterative refinement of such hypotheses.

Complementing this work, \textcite{yang2025moose} introduced the \modelname{Moose-Chem} framework to evaluate the novelty of \gls{llm}-generated hypotheses. 
To avoid data contamination, their benchmark exclusively uses papers published after the knowledge cutoff date of the evaluated model, \modelname{GPT-4o}. 
Ground-truth hypotheses were derived from articles in high-impact journals (e.g., Nature, Science) and validated by domain-specialized PhD researchers.
By iteratively providing the model with context from prior studies, \modelname{GPT-4o} achieved coverage of over $80\%$ of the evaluation set’s hypotheses while accessing only $4\%$ of the retrieval corpus, demonstrating efficient synthesis of ideas presumably not present in its training corpus.

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[!ht]
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \begin{figure}[H]
    \DIFaddendFL \centering
        \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure13.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Overview of \gls{llm}-based hypothesis generation}. 
    Current methods are based on \gls{llm}-sampling methods in which an \gls{llm} proposes new hypotheses. 
    The generated hypotheses are evaluated in terms of novelty and impact either by another \gls{llm} or by a human. Then, through experimentation, the hypotheses are transformed into results which showcase that current \glspl{llm} cannot produce groundbreaking ideas, limited to their training corpus, resulting in the best cases, in incremental work. This is shown metaphorically with the puzzle. The \enquote{pieces of chemical knowledge} based on the hypothesis produced by \glspl{llm} are already present in the \enquote{chemistry puzzle}, not unveiling new parts of it.}
    \label{fig:hypothesis-generation}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Are LLMs Actually Capable of Novel Hypothesis Generation?}

Automatic hypothesis generation is often regarded as the Holy Grail of automating the scientific process \autocite{coley2020autonomous}. However, achieving this milestone remains challenging, as generating novel and impactful ideas requires questioning current scientific paradigms \autocite{Kuhn1962Structure}---a skill typically refined through years of experience---which is currently impossible for most \gls{ml} systems.

Current progress in \gls{ml} illustrates these limitations \autocite{kon2025exp0bench0, gu2024interesting}. Although some studies claim success\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, as }\DIFaddend \gls{ai}-generated ideas \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{being }\DIFaddend accepted at workshops in \gls{ml} conferences via double-blind review \autocite{zhou2025tempest0}, these achievements are limited. 
First, accepted submissions often focus on coding tasks, one of the strongest domains for \glspl{llm}. Second, workshop acceptances are less competitive than main conferences, as they prioritize early-stage ideas over rigorously validated contributions. 
In chemistry, despite some works showing promise on these systems \autocite{yang2025moose0chem20}, \glspl{llm} struggle to propose \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{functional }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{innovative }\DIFaddend hypotheses \autocite{si2025ideation1execution}.
Their apparent success often hinges on extensive sampling and iterative refinement, rather than genuine conceptual innovation.

As \textcite{Kuhn1962Structure} argued, generating groundbreaking ideas demands challenging prevailing paradigms---a capability missing in current \gls{ml} models (they are trained to make the existing paradigm more likely in training rather than questioning their training data), as shown in \Cref{fig:hypothesis-generation}. 
Thus, while accidental discoveries can arise from non-programmed events (e.g., Fleming’s identification of penicillin \autocite{Fleming1929antibacterial, Fleming1945penicillin}), transformative scientific advances typically originate from deliberate critique of existing knowledge \autocite{popper1959logic, Lakatos1970falsification}. In addition, very often breakthroughs can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{also }\DIFdelend not be achieved by optimizing for a simple metric---as we often do not fully understand the problem and, hence, cannot design a metric.\autocite{stanley2015greatness}
Despite some publications suggesting that \gls{ai} scientists already exist, such claims are supported only by narrow evaluations that yield incremental progress \autocite{novikov2025alphaevolve}, not paradigm-shifting insights. For \gls{ai} to evolve from research assistants into autonomous scientists, it must demonstrate efficacy in addressing societally consequential challenges, such as solving complex, open-ended problems at scale (e.g., \enquote{millennium} math problems
\autocite{Carlson2006millennium}).

Finally, ethical considerations become critical as hypothesis generation grows more data-driven and automated. Adherence to legal and ethical standards must guide these efforts (see \Cref{sec:safety}) \autocite{danish_gov2024hypothesis}.


\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{With a hypothesis in hand, the next step is often to run an experiment to test it.
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Limitations}}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Current }\gls{llm}\DIFadd{-driven systems lack the kind of creativity needed for paradigm-shifting hypotheses, tending to rearrange training data and retrieved content rather than propose genuinely new mechanisms. As a result, the evaluation of such outputs is also fragile, because using proxy metrics for }\enquote{novelty} \DIFadd{or }\enquote{impact} \DIFadd{that poorly track real scientific value can be deceiving. Finally, the problem’s open-ended nature (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:data-section}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) makes systematic benchmarking ill-posed.
}


\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Scalable Evaluation}}\DIFadd{: The open-ended assessment of a hypothesis's potential impact remains a core challenge, as current methods are difficult to scale, costly, and inefficient due to a heavy reliance on human input }\autocite{nie2025assessing}\DIFadd{.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{The Integration Gap}}\DIFadd{: A critical disconnect persists between hypothesis generation and automated experimental validation, especially in fields like chemistry.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{The Paradigm Limitation}}\DIFadd{: The underlying operational constraints of current modeling approaches inherently favor incremental progress over transformative breakthroughs.
}\end{itemize}

%DIF > 

\DIFaddend \subsection{Experiment Planning}
\label{sec:planning}
Before a human or robot can execute any experiments, a plan must be created. 
Planning can be formalized as the process of decomposing a high-level task into a structured sequence of actionable steps aimed at achieving a specific goal. 
The term planning is often confused with scheduling and \gls{rl}, which are closely related but distinct concepts. Scheduling is a more specific process focused on the timing and sequence of tasks. 
It ensures that resources are efficiently allocated, experiments are conducted in an optimal order, and constraints (such as lab availability, time, and equipment) are respected.\autocite{kambhampati2023llmplanning} 
\gls{rl} is about adapting and improving plans over time based on ongoing results.\autocite{chen2022deep}



\subsubsection{Conventional Planning} 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Early experimental planning in chemistry relied on human intuition and domain expertise. 
One example of this is retrosynthesis. 
Since the 1960s, systemslike }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{lhasa} \autocite{corey1972computer} %%%
\DIFdel{began automating retrosynthesis using hand-coded rules and heuristics}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{warr2014short}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
Later tools}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{Early chemical planning systems}\DIFaddend , such as \DIFaddbegin \gls{lhasa} \autocite{corey1972computer} \DIFadd{and }\DIFaddend \modelname{Chematica}\autocite{grzybowski2018chematica}, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{expanded these efforts by integrating larger template libraries and optimization strategies. As reaction data grew in volume and complexity, manual rule encoding became unsustainable. 
Platforms like ASKCOS}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{tu2025askcos} %%%
\DIFdel{integrated }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gnn} %%%
\DIFdel{and neural classifiers to predict reactivity and suggest conditions, enabling actionable synthetic routes. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{All applications, however, face the problem that planning is difficult because search spaces are combinatorially large and evaluating potential paths, in principle, requires a model that can perfectly predict the outcomes of different actions. Conventional approaches often rely on various forms of search }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{relied on simple rules and templates. In particular, \modelname{Chematica} used heuristic-guided graph search with rule-based transforms and scoring functions to prune and prioritize routes. Modern systems, like ASKCOS}\autocite{tu2025askcos}\DIFadd{, explicitly use search }\DIFaddend algorithms such as \gls{bfs}, \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{dfs}%%%
\DIFdel{, }\DIFdelend \gls{mcts}\autocite{segler2017towards} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. 
Those, however, are often still not efficient enough to tackle long-horizon planning for complex problems. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to explore the combinatorially large space. But these planning search algorithms remain inefficient for long-horizon or complex planning tasks. }\autocite{liu2024entropy, zhao2024efficient}
\DIFaddend 


\subsubsection{LLMs to Decompose Problems into Plans}
\glspl{gpm}, in particular \glspl{llm}, can potentially assist in planning with two modes of thinking. 
Deliberate \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(system-2-like) }\DIFdelend thinking can be used to score potential options or to decompose problems into plans. 
Intuitive \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(system-1-like) }\DIFdelend thinking can be used to efficiently prune search spaces. 
These two modes align with psychological frameworks known as system-1 \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(intuitive) }\DIFaddend and system-2 \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(deliberate) }\DIFaddend thinking. \autocite{kahneman2011thinking}
In the system-1 thinking, \glspl{llm} support rapid decision-making by leveraging heuristics and pattern recognition to quickly narrow down options. 
In contrast, system-2 thinking represents a slower, more analytical process, in which \glspl{llm} solve complex \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{tasks---such as logical reasoning and planning---by }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{tasks by }\DIFaddend explicitly generating step-by-step reasoning. \autocite{ji2025test}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Decomposing a goal into actionable milestones relies on }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:planning_example} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
shows how a }\gls{llm} \DIFadd{applies }\DIFaddend this deliberate, system-2-style reasoning \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, enabling the model to evaluate alternatives and structure plans effectively}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to decompose a chemical problem into a sequence of planned steps}\DIFaddend .
A variety of strategies have been proposed to improve the reasoning capabilities of \glspl{llm} during inference. 
Methods such as \gls{cot} and least-to-most prompting guide models to decompose\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{problems into interpretable steps, improving transparency and interpretability}\DIFdelend . 
However, their effectiveness in planning is limited by error accumulation and linear thinking patterns.\autocite{stechly2024chain}
To address these limitations, recent test-time strategies such as repeat sampling and tree search have been proposed\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{to enhance planning capabilities in }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm}%%%
\DIFdelend . 
Repeated sampling allows the model to generate multiple candidate reasoning paths, encouraging diversity in thought and increasing the chances of discovering effective subgoal decompositions. \autocite{wang2024planning}
Meanwhile, tree search methods like \gls{tot} and \gls{rap} treat reasoning as a structured search, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{also }\DIFdelend using algorithms like \gls{mcts} to explore and evaluate multiple solution paths, facilitating more global and strategic decision-making. \autocite{hao2023reasoning}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Beyond purely linguistic reasoning, }\DIFdelend \glspl{llm} have also been \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{used to interpret natural-language queries and to translate them into structured planning steps, as demonstrated by systems like }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{+P}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{liu2023llm} %%%
\DIFdel{and }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{-DP}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{dagan2023dynamic}%%%
\DIFdel{, which integrated }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{with classical planners to convert planning problems into }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{pddl}%%%
\DIFdel{.
}%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{have also been }\DIFdelend applied to generate structured procedures from limited observations. For example, in quantum physics, a model was trained to infer reusable experimental templates from measurement data, producing Python code that generalized across system sizes. \autocite{arlt2024meta0designing} 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This demonstrates how }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{can support scientific planning by synthesizing high-level protocols from low-level evidence, moving beyond symbolic reasoning to executable plan generation.
}\DIFdelend 

\DIFaddbegin \begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
        \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/Chemrev_planning_example.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{\DIFaddFL{An example of using }\gls{llm} \DIFaddFL{for chemical planning to decompose problem}}\DIFaddFL{. }\gls{llm} \DIFaddFL{decomposes a chemical problem into sequential steps with detailed procedures. We manually evaluate the plan step by step, and highlight the problematic steps in text boxes, each linked to the corresponding reason. Plan was generated by ChatGPT-5.
}}
    \label{fig:planning_example}
\end{figure}


\DIFaddend \subsubsection{Pruning of Search Spaces}

Pruning refers to the process of eliminating unlikely or suboptimal options during the search to reduce the computational burden.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Because the number of potential pathways can grow exponentially, exhaustive search may be computationally intensive. 
}\DIFdelend Classical planners employ heuristics, value functions, or logical filters to perform pruning\autocite{bonet2012action}. 
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{can emulate pruning through learned heuristics, intuitive judgment, or context-driven evaluation, }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{gao2025synergizing} %%%
\DIFdel{reflecting system-1 thinking.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFaddend \Cref{fig:planning} illustrates how \glspl{llm} can support experimental planning by \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{selectively pruning options .
Rule-based heuristics derived from domain knowledge can automatically discard routes involving unfavorable motifs, such as chemically strained rings or complex aromatic scaffolds. 
Meanwhile, }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{can emulate }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{pruning options by emulating }\DIFaddend an expert chemist's intuition by discarding synthetic routes that appear unnecessarily long, inefficient, or mechanistically implausible. 

To further enhance planning efficacy, \glspl{llm} can be augmented with external tools that estimate the feasibility or performance of candidate plans, enabling targeted pruning of the search space before costly execution. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In \modelname{ChemCrow}, the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{collaborated with specialized chemical tools with knowledge about molecular and reaction properites. While \modelname{ChemCrow} does not explicitly generate and prune a large pool of candidate plans, these tools serve as real-time evaluators that help the model avoid unfeasible or inefficient directions during synthesis or reaction planning.
}\DIFdelend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In addition to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Beyond }\DIFaddend external tools, \glspl{llm} can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{also engage in self-correction, a reflective strategy that identifies and prunes }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{self-correct by pruning }\DIFaddend flawed reasoning steps \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{within their own outputs. 
This introspective pruning supports more robust and coherent planning by discarding faulty intermediate steps before they affect final decisions. As such, self-correction offers a lightweight yet effective mechanism for narrowing the solution space in complex reasoning tasks. 
At the highest }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to produce more coherent plans. At a higher }\DIFaddend level of oversight, human-in-the-loop frameworks \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{introduce expert feedback to guide pruning decisions. 
The \modelname{ORGANA}system}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{such as \modelname{ORGANA}}\DIFaddend \autocite{darvish2025organa} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{integrated chemist feedback into the planning process, helping define }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{incorporate expert chemist feedback to refine }\DIFaddend goals, resolve ambiguities, and eliminate invalid directions. \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Example~\ref{example:chemcrow_organa} presents examples illustrating how planning extends to real laboratory practice.
}

\DIFaddend \begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
        \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure14.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{\gls{gpm}-guided retrosynthesis route planning and pruning}. \glspl{gpm} can systematically evaluate and prune retrosynthetic routes using multiple reasoning capabilities to discriminate between viable and problematic approaches. The partially overlapping arrows at the start of each route indicate multiple steps. \textbf{Route A}: This route was pruned by heuristic reasoning due to the unfavorable aromatic core construction.
    \textbf{Route B}: This route was selected as it successfully passes all \gls{gpm} planning checks, demonstrating optimal synthetic feasibility.
    \textbf{Route C}: This pathway was pruned by external tools due to the poor \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{region-selectivity }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{regio-selectivity }\DIFaddendFL of the oxidation step.
    \textbf{Route D}: This route was pruned based on learned intuition, as it represents an inefficient multistep pathway; the route could just start with phenol instead of synthesizing it.
}
    \label{fig:planning}
\end{figure}


\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Evaluation}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Limitations}\DIFaddend } 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{While pruning accelerates planning, its effectiveness depends on reliable evaluation---the ability to judge whether a candidate plan is valid or promising. 
However, evaluating planning quality is particularly challenging in scientific fields such as chemistry and biology. 
Many alternative plans may achieve the same goal, so evaluation is inherently ambiguous in the absence of a comprehensive world model.
In open-ended domains, evaluation is often conducted manually.
For example, \modelname{ChemCrow} }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{bran2024augmenting} %%%
\DIFdel{relied on expert review to assess the correctness and plausibility of generated outputs. 
More dynamic evaluations can be performed in simulated or real embodied environments }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{song2023llm, choi2024lota}%%%
\DIFdel{, offering interactive feedback on feasibility. In parallel, automatic }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:planning_example} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
demonstrates how }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{can generate sophisticated-looking plans that conceal critical flaws, making complex, long-horizon chemical planning tasks particularly difficult to execute reliably }\autocite{Cao2025LargeLM}\DIFadd{. 
A limitation is that they often produce outputs that appear chemically plausible but are invalid or unsafe, due to their optimization for linguistic plausibility rather than chemical correctness and their lack of mechanistic understanding}\autocite{Bran2023TransformersAL, Evans2021TruthfulAD}
\DIFadd{Second, errors can also propagate from external tools like retrosynthesis planners, whose data and algorithmic shortcomings constrain reliability }\autocite{Li2025ChemHASHA}\DIFadd{. 
Finally, a broader limitation is the lack of grounding in experimental feedback, which creates persistent gaps between theoretical planning and practical feasibility.
}

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Reasoning Complexity Beyond Knowledge Retrieval}}\DIFadd{: Complex chemistry problems require long, tightly interconnected chains of reasoning, where minor errors can cascade and require understanding of dynamic interactions such as temperature effects on molecular behavior. Current }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{lack effective reasoning structures to guide domain-specific reasoning. }\autocite{Ouyang2023StructuredCR, Tang2025ChemAgentSL} 
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Evaluation and Feedback Bottleneck}}\DIFadd{: Current }\DIFaddend evaluation methods are \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{emerging. 
For example, \modelname{BioPlanner}}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{o2023bioplanner}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{used }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{often performed manually or indirectly, either relying on expert review as in \modelname{ChemCrow} }\autocite{bran2024augmenting} \DIFadd{or on }\DIFaddend pseudocode-based \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{evaluation, comparing }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{-generated protocols to expert-written pseudocode representations to assess plausibility and correctness without requiring manual review or physical execution.}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{comparisons as in \modelname{BioPlanner}.}\autocite{o2023bioplanner} \DIFadd{Integrating feedback remains an open direction for improving the practical feasibility of generated plans.
}\end{itemize}
\DIFaddend 


\subsection{Experiment Execution}
Once an experimental plan is available, whether from a human scientist's idea or \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a sophisticated }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{an }\DIFaddend AI model, the next step is to execute it. 
Regardless of its source, the plan must be translated into concrete, low-level actions for execution. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{One of the main challenges of lab automation is to convert the high-level and abstract experimental plan into real-world operations carried out by the experimental hardware (liquid-handing systems, robotic arms, instruments, etc.). 
}\DIFdelend 

It is worth noting that, despite their methodological differences, executing experiments \textit{in silico} (running simulations or code) and \textit{in vitro} are not fundamentally different---both follow an essentially identical workflow: Plan $\rightarrow$ Instructions $\rightarrow$ Execution $\rightarrow$ Analysis. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In a computer simulation, a researcher writes a program (plan), which is then compiled or interpreted into machine code (instructions) for the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{cpu}%%%
\DIFdel{, executed to produce data, and finally the outputs are analyzed. 
In an automated laboratory, the scientist specifies a protocol (plan), which must be translated into instrument commands (instructions), executed on a robotic platform, followed by the analysis of sensor data or assay results. Both scenarios require careful translation of abstract steps into concrete actions, as well as further decision-making based on the acquired results.
}\DIFdelend 


The execution of \textit{in silico} experiments can be reduced to two essential steps: preparing input files and running the computational code; \glspl{gpm} can be used in both steps.\autocite{Liu2025ASA, Mendible‑Barreto2025DynaMate, Zou2025ElAgente, Campbell2025MDCrow} \textcite{Jacobs2025orca} found that using a combination of fine-tuning, \gls{cot} and \gls{rag} (see \Cref{sec:model_adaptation}) can improve the performance of \glspl{llm} in generating executable input files for the quantum chemistry software \emph{ORCA}\autocite{ORCA5}, while \textcite{Gadde2025chatbot} created \modelname{AutosolvateWeb}, an \gls{llm}-based platform that assists users in preparing input files for \gls{qmmm} simulations of explicitly solvated molecules and running them on a remote computer. 
Examples of \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{llm}\DIFaddend -based autonomous agents (see 
\Cref{sec:agents}) capable of performing the entire computational workflow (i.e., preparing inputs, executing the code, and analyzing the results) are \modelname{MDCrow} \autocite{Campbell2025MDCrow} (for molecular dynamics) and \modelname{El Agente Q} \autocite{Zou2025ElAgente} (for quantum chemistry).

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \Glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{can also assist in automating }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Emerging examples show }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{assisting in }\DIFaddend \textit{in vitro} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{experiments. We can draw parallels from programming }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{experiment automation. Programming }\DIFaddend language paradigms---compiled vs.\ interpreted (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{see }\DIFdelend \Cref{fig:exec}A)\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{---to better understand how }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{can be useful in different approaches of experiment automation . 
In compiled languages (like \modelname{C++}or \modelname{Fortran}) , the entire code is converted ahead of time by another program called the }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{compiler} %%%
\DIFdel{into binary machine code , which is directly executable by the hardware. In interpreted languages (like \modelname{Python}or \modelname{JavaScript}) , a program called the }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{interpreter} %%%
\DIFdel{reads the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{---provide a useful analogy for understanding different automation approaches.
}

\DIFadd{Compiled languages (\modelname{C++}, \modelname{Fortran}) convert entire programs to machine code before execution. Interpreted languages (\modelname{Python}, \modelname{JavaScript}) translate and execute }\DIFaddend instructions line-by-line \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{during runtime, translating and executing them on the fly. Compiled languages offer high }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{at runtime. The tradeoff is that compiled languages offer higher }\DIFaddend performance and early error detection \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, making them ideal for performance-critical systems, but they require a separate compilation step and are less flexible during development}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{but require separate compilation steps}\DIFaddend . Interpreted languages \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{are easier to use, debug, and modify on the fly, which makes them great for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{enable }\DIFaddend rapid development and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{scripting, but they generally }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{on-the-fly modification, but }\DIFaddend run slower and catch errors only \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at runtime.
Similarly, we can broadly categorize different approaches to experiment automation into two different groups: }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{compiled automation} %%%
\DIFdel{and }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{interpreted automation} %%%
\DIFdel{(see }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{during execution.
}

\DIFadd{Similarly, experiment automation follows two paradigms (}\DIFaddend \Cref{fig:exec}B)\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. In the compiled approach, the entire protocol is translated---either by a human or a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{: }\enquote{compiled automation} \DIFadd{translates entire protocols---by human or }\DIFaddend \gls{gpm}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{---to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{---into }\DIFaddend low-level instructions before execution\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, while in interpreted automation, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. }\enquote{Interpreted automation} \DIFadd{uses }\DIFaddend the \gls{gpm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{plays a central role, acting as the }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{interpreter} %%%
\DIFdel{and executing the protocol step by step.
As we show below, it can be instructive to use this perspective when discussing approaches to automate experiment execution with }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{as runtime interpreter, executing protocols step-by-step.
}\DIFaddend 


\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[p]
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \begin{figure}[H]
    \DIFaddendFL \centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure15+16.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Programming languages vs.\ lab automation. A) programming paradigms}: In compiled languages, the entire source code is translated ahead of time to machine code by the compiler. This stand-alone code is then given to the \gls{os}, which is responsible for scheduling and distributing tasks to the hardware. In interpreted languages, the interpreter reads and translates each line of the source code to machine code and hands it to the \gls{os} for execution. \textbf{B) automation paradigms}: In the compiled approach, a \gls{gpm} formalizes the protocol, a compiler, such as the \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{chempiler}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Chempiler}\DIFaddendFL \autocite{steiner2019organic}, translates the formalized protocol to hardware-specific low-level steps, which the controller then executes---a central hub tasked with scheduling and distributing commands to chemical hardware. In the interpreted approach, a \gls{gpm}, acting as the interpreter, first breaks down the protocol into specific steps, then sends them (via an \gls{api}) for execution one by one. The strength of interpreted systems is dynamic feedback: after the execution of each step, the \gls{gpm} receives a signal (e.g., data, errors), which can influence its behavior for the next steps.}
    \label{fig:exec}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Compiled Automation}
In \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the case of }\DIFdelend \enquote{compiled automation}, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the experiment protocol needs to be formalized in a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{protocols are formalized in }\DIFaddend high-level \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{or }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{dsl} %%%
\DIFdel{that describes exactly what operations to perform in what order}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{languages or }\glspl{dsl}\DIFaddend . A chemical compiler (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{or }\DIFdelend \enquote{chempiler}\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \autocite{steiner2019organic}%%%
\DIFdel{) then converts this high-level protocol }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \autocite{mehr2020universal}\DIFadd{) converts these }\DIFaddend into low-level \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{code for the specific lab hardware , which is then executed by robotic instruments , orchestrated by a controller (refer to the caption of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{hardware instructions, which robotic instruments then execute (}\DIFaddend \Cref{fig:exec}B). 

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Protocol Languages}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{While \modelname{Python} -based scripts are frequently used }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{While \modelname{Python} scripts serve }\DIFaddend as the \textit{de facto} protocol language\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{due to \modelname{Python}'s accessibility and flexibility, }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{pylabrobot,vriza2023polybot, wang2025polybot} %%%
\DIFdel{specialized languages (}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, specialized }\DIFaddend \glspl{dsl} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{) have also been developed to }\DIFdelend provide more structured \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and semantically rich representationsof experimental procedures}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{representations}\DIFaddend .\autocite{wang2022ulsa, ananthanarayanan2010biocoder, autoprotocol2023, Park2023CMDL} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{One of the prominent examples of such languages is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For example, }\DIFaddend \gls{chidl}\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \autocite{xdl2023spec}%%%
\DIFdel{, developed as part of the Chemputer architecture }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{steiner2019organic, mehr2020universal, hammer2021chemputation}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
}%DIFDELCMD < \gls{chidl} %%%
\DIFdel{uses a }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{json}%%%
\DIFdel{-like format, and the experimental protocol is described by defining \modelname{Reagents}, \modelname{Vessels}, etc, and using abstract chemical commands such as }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \autocite{mehr2020universal, xdl2023spec} \DIFadd{describes protocols using abstract commands (}\DIFaddend \modelname{Add}, \modelname{Stir}, \modelname{Filter}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, etc. In the next step, the \modelname{Chempiler} software takes this }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{) and chemical objects (\modelname{Reagents}, \modelname{Vessels}). The \modelname{Chempiler} translates }\DIFaddend \gls{chidl} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{script and a description of the physical connectivity and composition of the automated platform as a graph and translates it into }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{chasm} %%%
\DIFdel{which is specific to the platform (akin to machine code).
In practice, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{chidl}  %%%
\DIFdel{has been used to automate multi-step organic syntheses with yields comparable to manual experiments.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{mehr2020universal}  
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{scripts into platform-specific instructions based on the physical laboratory configuration.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Developing experimental protocols in a formal language is a non-trivial task, often requiring specialized }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Writing protocols in formal languages requires }\DIFaddend coding expertise. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Within the compiled approach, the role of the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{is to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Here, }\glspl{gpm} \DIFaddend translate natural-language protocols into \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{their formalized, }\DIFdelend machine-readable \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{counterparts.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{Lamas2024DSLXpert, jiang2024protocode, conrad2025lowering, inagaki2023robotic} %%%
\DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{Vaucher2020AutoExtraction} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
used an encoder-decoder transformer model to convert English experimental procedures to structured sequences of pre-defined synthesis actions (e.g., \modelname{MakeSolution}, \modelname{SetTemperature}, \modelname{Extract}). They pre-trained the model on $2$M sentence-action pairs extracted by a rule-based }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{nlp} %%%
\DIFdel{algorithm and then fine-tuned it on manually annotated samples to improve accuracy. 
The model achieved exact sentence-pair matching in $61\%$ of the test samples and had more than $75\%$ overlap in $82\%$ of them. 
Although this approach accelerates automated protocol extraction from chemical literature, the output format is not directly suitable for execution. 
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{formats.}\autocite{Lamas2024DSLXpert, jiang2024protocode, conrad2025lowering, inagaki2023robotic,Vaucher2020AutoExtraction}
\DIFaddend \textcite{Pagel2024LLMChemputer} introduced a multi-agent workflow (based on \modelname{GPT-4}) that can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{address this issue and }\DIFdelend convert unstructured chemistry papers into executable code. 
The first agent extracts all synthesis-relevant text, including supporting information; a procedure agent then sanitizes the data and tries to fill the gaps from chemical databases (using \gls{rag}); another agent translates procedures into \gls{chidl} and simulates them on virtual hardware; finally, a critique agent cross-checks the translation and fixes errors.

The example above shows one of the strengths of the compiled approach: it allows for pre-validation. 
The protocol can be simulated or checked for any errors before running on the actual hardware, ensuring safety. 
Another example of \gls{llm}-based validators for chemistry protocols is \modelname{CLAIRify}.\autocite{Yoshikawa2023CLAIRify} 
Leveraging an iterative prompting strategy, it uses \modelname{GPT‑3.5} to first translate the natural-language protocol into \gls{chidl} script, then automatically verifies its syntax and structure, identifies any errors, appends those errors to the prompt, and prompts the \gls{llm} again---iterating this process until a valid \gls{chidl} script is produced. 


\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Similar to how compiled software can be recompiled for different platforms, compiled automation is hardware-agnostic: by using appropriate compilation, a well-defined protocol can---at least in principle---be run on different robotic systems as long as they have the required capabilities.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{rauschen2024universal, strieth-kalthoff2024delocalized,wilbraham2021chemPU} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{In practice, however, inconsistencies in hardware interfaces and software standards across the lab automation community make cross-platform execution challenging.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The main limitations of compiled approaches are the flip side of their strengths: low flexibility and adaptability. 
Any logic or decision-making must either be explicitly encoded within the protocol---necessitating meticulous scripting---or delegated to an external control layer.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{mehr2023digitizing,leonov2024integrated} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{If something unexpected occurs (a pump clogging, a reaction taking longer than expected), the pre-compiled protocol cannot easily adjust in real-time, and human intervention or a complete recompile might be needed.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{Interpreted Automation}
Interpreted programming languages support higher \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{levels of abstraction , enabling the use of more general and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{abstraction levels through }\DIFaddend flexible command structures. Similarly, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{since }\DIFdelend \glspl{gpm} can translate high-level goals into concrete steps\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \autocite{ahn2022can, huang2022language}%%%
\DIFdel{,they can act as an }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{interpreter} %%%
\DIFdel{between the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,}\autocite{ahn2022can, huang2022language} \DIFadd{acting as }\enquote{interpreters} \DIFadd{between }\DIFaddend experimental intent and lab hardware.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For instance, given an instruction }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{For example, given }\DIFaddend \enquote{titrate the solution until it turns purple}, a \gls{gpm} agent (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{see }\DIFdelend \Cref{sec:agents}) can break \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{it down into smaller steps and convert each step to executable code, allowing it to perform incremental additions of titrant and read a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{this into executable steps at runtime: add titrant incrementally, read }\DIFaddend color sensor, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{looping until the condition is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{loop until condition }\DIFaddend met. This \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{conversion of concrete steps to code happens at runtime; it is not pre-compiled. 
We refer to such systems as }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is }\DIFaddend \enquote{interpreted automation}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{systems. 
In contrast to the deterministic, preplanned nature of compiled systems, interpreted architectures introduce }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{---conversion happens during execution, not before.
}

\DIFadd{The key advantage is }\DIFaddend real-time decision-making. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{As each actioncompletes}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{After each action}\DIFaddend , the system \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{collects }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{analyzes }\DIFaddend sensor data (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{instrument }\DIFdelend readings, spectra, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{error messages, etc.) which the agent analyzes and decides on the next action. This allows for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{errors) and selects the next step. This enables }\DIFaddend dynamic branching and conditional logic \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{during the experiment execution}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{impossible in pre-compiled protocols}\DIFaddend .

\modelname{Coscientist}\autocite{boiko2023autonomous} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is an }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{-based chemistry assistant built around \modelname{GPT-4} that can autonomously design and execute experiments. 
It can take high-level goals and call tools to write code in real-time in order to control an Opentrons OT-2 }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{demonstrates interpreted automation using \modelname{GPT-4} to control }\DIFaddend liquid-handling \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{robot. The architecture included a web-search module, a documentation module (to read instrument manuals), a \modelname{Python} execution module (to run generated code in a sandbox), and an experiment execution module that sends code to actual lab equipment. If an error occurred, the system would get feedback and \modelname{GPT-4} would debug }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{robots. The system searches the web for protocols, reads instrument documentation, writes \modelname{Python} code in real-time, and executes experiments on physical hardware. When errors occur, \modelname{GPT-4} debugs }\DIFaddend its own code. \modelname{Coscientist} successfully \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{planned and executed multistep syntheses with minimal human intervention. 
For example, it efficiently optimized a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{optimized }\DIFaddend palladium cross-coupling \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{reaction with minimal human input, outperforming a standard Bayesian optimizer baseline }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reactions, outperforming Bayesian optimization }\DIFaddend in finding high-yield conditions.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Another example is }\DIFdelend \modelname{ChemCrow}\autocite{bran2024augmenting} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, a \modelname{GPT-4} -based agent augmented }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{augments \modelname{GPT-4} }\DIFaddend with $18$ expert-designed tools for tasks \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{like }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{including }\DIFaddend compound lookup, spectral analysis, and retrosynthesis. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{ChemCrow} can perform tasks across synthesis planning, drug discovery, and materials design by invoking external software for things like retrosynthesis, property prediction, database queries, etc. 
}\DIFdelend It planned and executed \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the syntheses of an insect repellent, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{syntheses of }\DIFaddend \gls{deet} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, and three different organocatalysts }\DIFdelend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{even guided the discovery of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{three thiourea organocatalysts (example~\ref{example:chemcrow_organa}), and collaborated with chemists to discover }\DIFaddend a new chromophore\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{dye}\DIFdelend .


\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The interpreted paradigm is highly generalizable; in principle, the same }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{agent controlling a chemistry experiment could be re-purposed to a biology or materials experiment with minimal reprogramming because it operates at the level of intent and semantic understanding. 
However, fully autonomous labs featuring interpreted automation are still experimental themselves---ensuring their reliability and accuracy remains an open challenge. 
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Despite being labeled as }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{autonomous,} %%%
\DIFdel{both systems mentioned above often need prompting nudges and human correction. 
In addition, these models can replicate known procedures and use databases, but they lack an understanding of mechanisms or underlying principles. Another issue is full reproducibility and long-term experiment tracking. Since the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{'s response might not be deterministic, small changes in prompts can yield different results and closed-source models like \modelname{GPT-4} can change over time. 
Hallucinations remain a risk, especially in planning complex or sensitive reactions. 
In addition, allowing an agent to control hardware brings safety considerations; the flexibility of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{means that they can devise unanticipated actions. Designing safety nets for these systems is an active area of research. (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:safety}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
)
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{Hybrid Approaches}
Between \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the two extremes of fully compiled vs.\ }\DIFdelend fully \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{compiled and fully }\DIFaddend interpreted automation lies a hybrid approach that \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{seeks to combine the best of both paradigms: the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{combines the }\DIFaddend safety and reliability of compiled protocols \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai}%%%
\DIFdel{-driven }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{with the AI-driven }\DIFaddend flexibility of interpreted systems. \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The key difference from purely interpreted systems is that during each experiment run , the plan is fixed , ensuring }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Each experiment run follows a fixed plan for }\DIFaddend safety and reproducibility, but between runs, the plan can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{dynamically change }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{adapt }\DIFaddend based on the \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{GPM}\DIFaddend ’s interpretation of results. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Once the initial plan (ideally devised by the same }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{in a previous step) is provided to a hybrid system, instead of reducing it to smaller steps and directly sending the instructions to a laboratory one at a time, the protocol is first formalized---i.e., it is translated to a formal machine-readable format such as }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{chidl}%%%
\DIFdel{. Once validated, the formalized protocol is compiled and executed. After the completion of execution, the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{receives the results and decides what experiment to perform next. This cycle repeats, creating an autonomous optimization or discovery loop. 
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{This hybrid strategy is attractive because it provides a safety net against mistakes made by the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{interpreter ; any generated procedure must pass through a formalization and verification stage before real execution, and therefore, erroneous or hallucinated steps can be caught. For example, if the interpreter hallucinated adding \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\SI{1000}{\milli\liter} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
of a solvent but the hardware has only \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\SI{100}{\milli\liter} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
capacity, it can be flagged as an error}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{This design provides a safeguard against interpreter errors, since every generated procedure passes through formal verification before execution---catching issues like a hallucinated instruction to add \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\SI{1000}{\milli\liter} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
of solvent to a \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\SI{100}{\milli\liter} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
flask}\DIFaddend .


\modelname{ORGANA} \autocite{darvish2025organa} is an \gls{llm}-based robotic assistant following this hybrid paradigm. 
It allows human chemists to describe their experimental goal in natural language. The system can converse with the user to clarify ambiguous requests (the agent would ask \enquote{do you mean X or Y?} if the instructions are unclear). Once the goal is understood, it uses \modelname{CLAIRify} \autocite{Yoshikawa2023CLAIRify} to convert and validate the natural-language description of a chemistry experiment into a \gls{chidl} script, which can be executed on a compatible platform. 
In one case, \modelname{ORGANA} carried out a multistep electrochemistry procedure---polishing electrodes, running an experiment, and analyzing the data---involving 19 substeps that it coordinated in parallel \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(see example~\ref{example:chemcrow_organa})}\DIFaddend .
If an unexpected observation occurred (e.g., a solution does not change color when expected), the system can notice via image analysis and modify the plan or alert the user. 
In user studies, \modelname{ORGANA} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{significantly }\DIFdelend reduced the manual labor and frustration for chemists, who could offload tedious tasks and trust the agent to handle low-level decisions.
