\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Limitations}}
\DIFadd{Current automation systems remain prototypes. 
}

\DIFadd{Interpreted systems require frequent human intervention despite autonomy claims. They replicate known procedures but lack mechanistic understanding. Non-deterministic }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{responses create reproducibility issues---small prompt changes yield different results, and closed-source models evolve unpredictably. Hallucinations risk incorrect planning for complex reactions. Hardware control introduces safety concerns: flexible }\DIFaddend \glspl{gpm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{can misinterpret ambiguous instructions}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{can devise unanticipated actions}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{leading to invalid or unsafe procedures.
This problem is compounded by the lack of universally adopted standards for protocol formalization ; while }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{requiring robust safeguards (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:safety}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
).
}

\DIFadd{Compiled systems offer reliability but require extensive upfront formalization. The effort to translate protocols into formal languages often outweighs automation benefits for typical laboratory workflows.
}





\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}
\DIFadd{Self-driving laboratories orchestrated by }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{face technical challenges requiring research advances:}\autocite{Tom2024SDL,Seifrid2022SDL}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Grounding Natural Language to Laboratory Actions.}} \DIFadd{Translating ambiguous natural-language instructions (}\enquote{heat gently}\DIFadd{) into precise, safe operations requires developing validation layers that detect physically impossible or hazardous actions before hardware execution.
}

\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Universal Protocol Standards.}} \DIFadd{No widely adopted formalization standard exists. While }\DIFaddend languages like \gls{chidl} show promise, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{inconsistencies in abstraction , device compatibility, and community uptake limit interoperability.
Real-time execution adds further complexity, as systems must detect and respond to failuresor unexpected behaviors; however, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{achieving interoperability across platforms requires community consensus on abstraction levels and device interfaces. }\Glspl{mcp} \DIFadd{offer a potential path forward by enforcing consistent interfaces between }\glspl{gpm}\DIFadd{, instruments, and verification layers.
}

\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Autonomous Error Recovery.}} \DIFadd{Current systems cannot autonomously diagnose and recover from experimental failures. Developing }\DIFaddend general-purpose \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{validation }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{failure detection }\DIFaddend mechanisms and recovery strategies \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{remain underdeveloped.
Hardware integration is another bottleneck; current commercial robotic platforms are prohibitively expensive and lab environments often rely on a patchwork of instruments with proprietary interfaces, and building robust, unified control layers demands considerable engineering overhead. 
Another challenge is multi-modality in chemistry; chemists use a wide variety of data (e.g., spectra}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{would enable truly autonomous operation.
}

\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Multimodal Integration.}} \DIFadd{Chemists use diverse data types---spectra, chromatograms}\DIFaddend , TLC plates, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{SEM images). Without integrating these forms of output, models will be limited in their decision-making}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and microscopy images}\DIFaddend . \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Finally, ensuring reproducibility and regulatory compliance requires that every step be logged , validated, and traceable at the level required for clinical or industrial adoption (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:safety}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. These challenges must be addressed in tandem to move from experimental demonstrations toward reliable, scalable, and trustworthy autonomous laboratories.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Integrating these modalities into }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{decision-making loops remains technically challenging but essential for human-level experimental reasoning.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Verification and Provenance.}} \DIFadd{Industrial and clinical applications require complete experimental provenance: every decision logged with reasoning traces, all parameters recorded, and outcomes traceable to specific model versions (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:safety}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
).
}\end{itemize}

\DIFaddend \subsection{Data Analysis}
The analysis of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{spectroscopic and }\DIFdelend experimental data in chemistry remains a predominantly manual process. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Even seemingly straightforward steps, such as plotting or summarizing results, demand repeated manual intervention.
}\DIFdelend 

One key challenge that makes automation particularly difficult is the extreme heterogeneity of chemical data sources. 
Laboratories often rely on a wide variety of instruments, some of which are decades old, rarely standardized, or unique in configuration.\autocite{jablonka2022making} 
These devices output data in incompatible, non-standardized, or poorly documented formats, each requiring specialized processing pipelines. 
Despite efforts like \modelname{JCAMP-DX} \autocite{McDonald1988standard}, standardization attempts remain \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{scarce }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{limited }\DIFaddend and have generally failed to gain widespread use. 
This diversity makes rule-based or hard-coded solutions largely infeasible, as they cannot generalize across the long tail of edge cases and exceptions found in real-world workflows.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{However, this exact complexity makes data analysis in chemistry a promising candidate }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{This complexity makes chemical data analysis a promising application }\DIFaddend for \glspl{gpm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. 
They are designed to operate flexibly across }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:anaylsis}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). These models handle }\DIFaddend diverse tasks and formats \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, relying on implicit knowledge captured }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{using implicit knowledge }\DIFaddend from broad training data. In other domains, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{narayan2022can} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
showed that models like \modelname{GPT-3 DaVinci} can already perform classical data processing tasks such as cleaning, transformation, and error detection through prompting alone. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{kayali2023chorus} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
introduced \modelname{Chorus} that shows that }\DIFdelend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{can analyze }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{have successfully processed }\DIFaddend heterogeneous tabular data \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and performed classical data analysis }\DIFaddend without task-specific training.\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{Chorus} demonstrates that by converting tables into a standardized text format and using zero-shot prompting (i.e., prompts with no examples), }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{can flexibly analyze tables even when they differ in structure, column names, or data types.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \autocite{narayan2022can, kayali2023chorus}
\DIFaddend 

\begin{figure}[!ht]
    \centering
\includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure17.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Static conventional data analysis workflow vs.\ dynamic \gls{gpm} generated workflow}. \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{The chemical }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Chemical }\DIFaddendFL analysis can be \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{done }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{performed }\DIFaddendFL with a variety of \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{possible }\DIFdelendFL instruments and techniques, resulting in \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{a large number of }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{many }\DIFaddendFL possible output data formats. The \gls{gpm} can use these diverse, raw data and process \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{it }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{them }\DIFaddendFL into easy-to-understand plots, analysis\DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, }\DIFaddendFL and reports. A hard-coded workflow, in contrast, is specifically made to analyze one specific data format and spectra and produces a fixed output format, e.g., the \gls{smiles} of the analyzed molecule.}
    \label{fig:anaylsis}
\end{figure}

\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Prompting}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Initial evaluations demonstrated }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In chemistry, however, only a handful of studies have so far demonstrated similar capabilities. Early evaluations showed }\DIFaddend that \glspl{gpm} can support basic \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{data analysis workflows . }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{Fu2025large} 
%DIFDELCMD <  %%%
\DIFdel{For example, in chemistry, this enabled }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{workflows such as }\DIFaddend the classification of \gls{xps} signals \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \autocite{decurt2024large} %%%
\DIFdelend based on peak positions \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, intensities, or characteristic spectral patterns).}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and intensities.}\autocite{Fu2025large,decurt2024large} 
 \DIFaddend 

Spectroscopic data \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{are not always available in structured textual form. 
In many practical cases, it appears }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{often appear }\DIFaddend as raw plots or images, making direct interpretation by \glspl{vlm} a more natural starting point for automated analysis. 
A broad assessment of \gls{vlm}-based spectral analysis was introduced with the \modelname{MaCBench} benchmark \autocite{alampara2024probing}, which systematically evaluates how \glspl{vlm} interpret experimental data in chemistry and materials science---including various types of spectra such as \gls{ir}, \gls{nmr}, and \gls{xrd}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{q---directly }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{---directly }\DIFaddend from images. They showed that while \glspl{vlm} can correctly extract isolated features from plots, the performance substantially drops in tasks requiring deeper spatial reasoning.
To overcome these limitations, \textcite{kawchak2024high} explored two-step pipelines that decouple visual perception from chemical reasoning. First, the model interprets each spectrum individually (e.g., converting  \gls{ir}, \gls{nmr}, or \gls{ms} images into textual peak descriptions), and second, a \gls{llm} analyzes these outputs to propose a molecular structure based on the molecular formula. 


\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Agentic Systems}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Beyond zero-shot prompting of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{, one can develop agentic systems that combine multiple analysis steps end-to-end. 
In this regard}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{More complex agentic systems extend beyond single-step analysis and attempt to orchestrate entire workflows. 
For example}\DIFaddend , \textcite{ghareeb2025robin0} developed \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{Robin}---a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{a }\DIFaddend multi-agent system for assisting biological research with hypothesis generation (see \Cref{fig:hypothesis-generation}) and experimental analysis. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Its }\DIFaddend data analysis agent \modelname{Finch} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{performs autonomous analysis of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{autonomously processes }\DIFaddend raw or preprocessed \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{experimental }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{biological }\DIFaddend data, such as \gls{rna} sequencing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and flow cytometry. 
Given a user prompt (e.g., }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{\gls{rna} sequencing differential expression analysis}%%%
\DIFdel{), \modelname{Finch} executes code in a Jupyter notebook to process the data, apply relevant statistical methods, and generate }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{or flow cytometry, by executing code in Jupyter notebooks and producing }\DIFaddend interpretable outputs. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For flow cytometry, this includes gating strategies and significance testing, while for }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{rna} %%%
\DIFdel{sequencing, it encompasses differential expression and gene ontology enrichment analysis. 
}\DIFdelend Currently, only these two data types are supported, and expert-designed prompts are still required to ensure reliable results. 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Recent work extends agentic systems beyond single-step data evaluation toward executing and optimizing entire workflows. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Similarly, }\DIFaddend \textcite{mandal2024autonomous} introduced \modelname{AILA}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(Artificially Intelligent
Lab Assistant) utilizing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, which utilizes }\DIFaddend \gls{llm}-agents to plan, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{code, }\DIFdelend execute, and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{revise complete }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{iteratively refine full }\DIFaddend \gls{afm} analysis pipelines. \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Compared to earlier prototypes, this systems emphasize transparency and reproducibility by producing both code and reports.
}\DIFaddend The system handles tasks such as image processing, defect detection, clustering, and \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the }\DIFaddend extraction of physical parameters. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Compared to systems like \modelname{Finch}, \modelname{AILA} shifts the focus from generating summaries to performing and improving full experimental analyses with minimal user input while maintaining transparency and reproducibility through code and reports.
}\DIFdelend 

\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Current }\DIFdelend Limitations}
While \glspl{gpm} offer promising capabilities for automating scientific data analysis, several \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{concrete }\DIFaddend limitations remain. 
Recent evaluations such as \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{FMs4Code} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\modelname{SciCode} }\DIFaddend \autocite{tian2024scicode} have shown that even \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{state-of-the-art models like \modelname{GPT-4-Turbo} and \modelname{Claude 2} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sota} \DIFadd{like \modelname{GPT-4-Turbo} }\DIFaddend frequently produce syntactically correct but semantically incorrect code\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{when tasked with }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, for instance, in }\DIFaddend common data analysis steps \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, }\DIFdelend such as reading files, applying filters, or generating plots.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Typical issues include incorrect column usage, or inconsistent output formatting.
}\DIFdelend 

These technical shortcomings are \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{reinforced by the model's }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{further amplified by }\DIFaddend sensitivity to prompt formulation. As demonstrated by \textcite{Yan2020auto} and \textcite{alampara2024probing}, \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{even }\DIFaddend minor changes in wording or structure can lead to \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{significantly different outputs}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{drastically different results}\DIFaddend , highlighting a lack of robustness in prompt-based control. 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Together, these findings suggest that while foundation models can generalize across diverse data formats and analysis types, their current performance is not yet sufficient for fully autonomous use in scientific analysis settings. 
Robust }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In practice, this means that robust }\DIFaddend prompting strategies, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{post-generation }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{systematic }\DIFaddend validation, and human oversight remain essential components \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in practice. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{of any current deployment.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}
\DIFadd{Looking forward, several open challenges remain unresolved.
}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{True Chemical Reasoning}}\DIFadd{: It remains unclear whether current }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{can perform genuine chemical analysis rather than relying on pattern-matching or shallow feature extraction. }\autocite{alampara2024probing, alampara2025task}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Seamless Laboratory Integration}}\DIFadd{: No commercial systems yet provide robust, end-to-end interoperability with the diverse analytical instruments used in chemistry laboratories. Existing research prototypes support only limited data types and still depend heavily on expert curation. }\autocite{ghareeb2025robin0, mandal2024autonomous}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Standardization and Real-World Validation}}\DIFadd{: Achieving production-ready systems requires progress not only in model robustness but also in data and protocol standardization, hardware integration, and thorough testing under realistic laboratory conditions. }\autocite{testini2025measuring}
\end{itemize}


\DIFaddend \subsection{Reporting}
To share insights obtained from data analysis, one often converts them into scientific \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{reports. 
Also, in }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{publications or other forms of content, such as reports or blogs.
In }\DIFaddend this step, \glspl{gpm} can \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{also }\DIFaddend take a central role\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, which we discuss in the following. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Reporting refers to converting scientific results into shareable reports, scientific publications, blogs, and other forms of content. 
This section describes two main applications of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{in scientific reporting: converting data into explanations and the first steps towards using these models as fully-fledged writing assistants}\DIFdelend . \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{While writing assistance has been showcased in past works, it remains limited in scope and real-world impact.
}\DIFaddend 


\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{From Data to Explanation}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{From Data to Explanation}} \DIFaddend The lack of explainability of \gls{ml} predictions generates skepticism among experimental chemists\autocite{wellawatte2025human}, hindering the wider adoption of such models.\autocite{wellawatte2022model}
One promising approach to address this challenge is to convey explanations of model predictions in natural language. 
An approach proposed by \textcite{wellawatte2025human} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is to couple }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{involves coupling }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} with feature importance analysis tools, such as \gls{shap} or \gls{lime}. 
In this framework, \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{can additionally interact with tools such as }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the }\gls{llm} \DIFadd{performs three key functions: First, it translates technical feature names into more accessible language. Second, using }\DIFaddend \gls{rag} over \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{arxiv} to provide evidence-based explanations.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{scientific literature, it retrieves relevant excerpts that explain the physicochemical relationships between identified molecular features and target properties. Third, it synthesizes these components into coherent natural language explanations that not only identify which structural features correlate with the property of interest, but also hypothesize }\textit{\DIFadd{why}} \DIFadd{these relationships exist based on established chemical principles from the literature. 
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Writing Assistance}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{When considering }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ml}%%%
\DIFdel{-based assistance in scientific writing, we can distinguish two primary modes: systems that aid authors during the active writing process and tools that optimize or refine scientific articles after initial drafting. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The former refers to the use of writing copilots that can suggest }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Writing Assistance}} \glspl{llm} \DIFadd{can assist with }\DIFaddend syntax improvement, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{identify text redundancies}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{redundancy identification}\DIFaddend ,\autocite{khalifa2024using} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{caption figures and tables}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{hsu2021scicap,selivanov2023medical}%%%
\DIFdel{,or provide }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{figure and table captioning,}\autocite{hsu2021scicap,selivanov2023medical} \DIFaddend caption-figure \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{match evaluation}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{hsu2023gpt04}%%%
\DIFdel{,but also more specific applications like writing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{matching,}\autocite{hsu2023gpt04} \DIFadd{and }\DIFaddend alt-text \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(descriptive text that explains the meaning and purpose of an image in digital content)}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{singh2024figura11y}%%%
\DIFdel{.}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{generation.}\autocite{singh2024figura11y} \DIFadd{Models can be personalized for specific audiences or writing styles.}\autocite{li2023teach}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Under the latter mode, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{can be used to assist non-native English speakers with scientific writing }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{giglio2023use}%%%
\DIFdel{.It could even allow authors to write in their native language and use }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{for communicating scientific results in English.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Another application of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{is to assist with completing checklistsbefore submitting a publication. For example, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{goldberg2024usefulness} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
benchmark the use of }\DIFdelend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in completing the author checklist for the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{neurips} %%%
\DIFdel{2025. They concluded that }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{has also been shown to potentially help complete submission checklists. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{goldberg2024usefulness} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
found }\DIFaddend $70\%$ of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the authors found the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{neurips} \DIFadd{2025 authors found }\DIFaddend \gls{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{-assistant useful }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{assistance useful for checklist completion}\DIFaddend , with the same fraction \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{indicating they would revise their own checklist based on the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{revising their submissions based on }\DIFaddend model feedback.

\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Vision}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Limitations}\DIFaddend }
\DIFaddbegin \gls{llm} \DIFadd{explanations appear credible but often lack faithfulness to underlying reasoning.}\autocite{agarwal2024faithfulness} \DIFadd{Models can reinforce existing biases through training data or prompting strategies.}\autocite{kobak2025delving} \DIFadd{While }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{can process large datasets, they miss subtle artifacts and anomalies human researchers detect, and struggle distinguishing correlation from causation.}\autocite{jin2023large}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Few have ventured into fully automating the writing process.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{yamada2025ai} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{While at its inception, reporting using }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{has tremendous potential. 
In \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:writing_with_ml} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
we showcase how the future of reporting could look like if we were to integrate }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{at each step of the process.}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Provenance Tracking Systems.}} \DIFadd{Developing methods to trace every claim back to specific training examples or retrieved sources. This requires architectures that maintain explicit links between generated text and source materials, enabling verification of attribution completeness.
    }\DIFaddend 

    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[!ht]
%DIFDELCMD <     \centering
%DIFDELCMD <         \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure18.pdf}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\textbf{\DIFdelFL{Vision for }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdelFL{in reporting, a visualization of the scientific writing process}}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{. }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdelFL{can be used at every stage of the process. For creating the pre-print, we can utilize the multimodal capabilities of these models to write detailed captions for figures. For the peer-review process, we can harness the ability of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdelFL{to summarize and prioritize information (e.g., design a time-efficient plan to address the peer review). When converting a document from a peer-reviewed pre-print, we often need to implement the publisher's requirements. In this case, we can make use of agentic systems that would assist with minor text fixes or document restructuring.}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:writing_with_ml}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{figure}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{An idea entertained by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{li2023teach} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
in the context of education is personalized writing.However, it is still widely unexplored in its goal: to make science accessible to everyone. 
A personalized model that learns user preferences and domain expertise can be used to deliver the message of a scientific article in simpler terms. 
As a result, we might observe a rise in cross-domain scientific collaborations and a rising interest in science.}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\section{\DIFdel{Accelerating Applications}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{section}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The application of accelerated approaches in the scientific discovery cycle (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:applications}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) hinges on their ability to streamline and enhance each stage of the process. However, a fundamental challenge in effectively implementing these approaches lies in the choice of machine-readable representation.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{This challenge is particularly evident in the representation of molecules and materials, which must balance computational efficiency with the preservation of structural, compositional, and functional properties.
    Take, for example, the high-temperature superconductor \ce{YBa2Cu3O_{7-x}}. 
While atomic positions and coordinates are theoretically sufficient to solve the Schrödinger equation and describe this material, such a representation may not provide the adaptability necessary for diverse tasks. What defines a good representation depends on the problem. }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{huang2016understanding}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
A representation designed to predict critical temperature must efficiently encode the relationship between oxygen stoichiometry and superconducting properties, emphasizing features like oxygen vacancy patterns and charge transfer mechanisms. 
Conversely, a representation for structural stability might prioritize different geometric or bonding characteristics.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{This tension has led to three primary strategies for representing molecules and materials (read \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:common_representations} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
to learn in detail about the different representations that currently exist). First, domain-specific text-based formats---such as }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} \autocite{weininger1988smiles}%%%
\DIFdel{, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{selfies} \autocite{krenn2020self}%%%
\DIFdel{, and }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{cif} \autocite{hall1991crystallographic}%%%
\DIFdel{---offer compact, machine-readable encodings of structural information. While these necessarily omit certain physical details, their computational tractability has enabled breakthroughs, as demonstrated by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{jablonka2024leveraging} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
in their }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Authorship Frameworks.}} \DIFadd{Defining contribution taxonomies that specify when }\DIFaddend \gls{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{-based generation of valid molecular and material structures.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{use constitutes co-authorship versus tool use. Journals and institutions need consensus guidelines for disclosure, attribution, and accountability when }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{assist in research reporting.
}\end{itemize}
\DIFaddend 


\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Yet, the question remains: Which representation is optimal for a given task? Future advances in accelerated discovery will likely hinge on adaptive representations that dynamically balance these competing demands.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \section{\DIFadd{Accelerating Applications}}
\DIFaddend \subsection{Property Prediction} \label{sec:prediction}

\glspl{gpm} have emerged as a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{powerful }\DIFdelend tool for predicting molecular and material properties\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, offering an alternative to traditional quantum mechanical calculations or specialized }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ml} %%%
\DIFdel{models. 
Current }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. 
Current examples of }\DIFaddend \gls{gpm}-driven property prediction \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{tasks }\DIFdelend span both classification and regression \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. 
Unlike conventional approaches that rely on task-specific architectures and extensively labeled data, }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{have demonstrated strong generalization capabilities across diverse domains, efficiently adapting to various prediction tasks. 
Their success extends to multiple datasets, }\DIFdelend from standardized benchmarks such as \modelname{MoleculeNet} \autocite{wu2018moleculenet}, to curated datasets targeting specific applications such as antibacterial activity \autocite{chithrananda2020chemberta} or photovoltaic efficiency \autocite{aneesh2025semantic}.

Three key methodologies have been explored to adapt \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{these models }\DIFaddend for property prediction: prompting techniques (see \Cref{sec:prompting}), fine-tuning (see \Cref{sec:fine-tuning}) on domain-specific data, and \gls{rag} (see \Cref{sec:rag}) approaches that combine \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{models }\DIFaddend with external knowledge bases.

\subsubsection{Prompting} 
Prompt engineering involves designing targeted instructions to guide \glspl{gpm} in performing specialized tasks without altering their underlying parameters\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{by leveraging their embedded knowledge}\DIFdelend .
In molecular and materials science, this strategy goes beyond simply asking a model to predict properties. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{It also includes carefully structured prompts to elicit detailed molecular and material descriptions directly from the model's pre-trained knowledge.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{liu2025integrating} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
conducted a comprehensive evaluation of different prompting techniques to predict the properties of organic small molecules and crystal materials. 
Some of these techniques included }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Prompting strategies for molecular property prediction have evolved from foundational techniques like }\DIFaddend domain-knowledge \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(prior knowledge was embedded in the prompt), expert (role-play instructions), }\DIFdelend and few-shot \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{cot} %%%
\DIFdel{(the text}\textit{%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Let's think step by step}%%%
} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{is added) prompting. 
Of these, domain knowledge achieved maximum performance. However, their evaluation was limited to a relatively small set of molecules and tasks, and the effectiveness of their domain-knowledge approach may not generalize to other molecular property domains.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Building on these foundational prompting strategies, few-shot prompting approaches leverage }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icl} %%%
\DIFdel{to enhance performance through selected examples \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{liu2024moleculargpt} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
used }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{string representations of molecules with few-shot }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icl}%%%
\DIFdel{, retrieving structurally similar molecules as demonstrations to enhance property prediction. 
This approach highlights how }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icl} %%%
\DIFdel{can transfer knowledge from similar molecule examples without requiring model fine-tuning for each task. 
However, the effectiveness of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icl} %%%
\DIFdel{depends on the quality of retrieved examples.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{fifty2023incontext} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
moved beyond direct text prompting of molecules and introduced }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{camp}%%%
\DIFdel{: an }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icl} %%%
\DIFdel{algorithm that uses a two-stage encoding approach without relying on pre-trained }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{.
First, a specialized }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{mpnn} %%%
\DIFdel{encodes molecule graphs into molecular embeddings rather than processing them as raw text. 
These embeddings are then fed into a transformer encoder, which learns contextualized representations across the support set (a small collection of labeled molecule-property pairs) and the unlabeled query molecules. They demonstrated }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{camp}%%%
\DIFdel{'s ability to outperform existing few-shot learning baselines by providing relevant molecular examples within the prompt context. However, this approach is constrained by the context-length limitations of the underlying }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{lm} %%%
\DIFdel{and the challenge of selecting optimal demonstration examples.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{More sophisticated approaches have leveraged prompting as part of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reasoning }\autocite{liu2025integrating} \DIFadd{to more advanced methods with }\DIFaddend multi-modal frameworks \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. The \modelname{LLM4SD} pipeline by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{zheng2025large} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
employs specialized prompts to guide }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{lm} %%%
\DIFdel{through their pre-trained knowledge on scientific literature, generating known rules (e.g., molecules weighing under \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\SI{500}{Da} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
are more likely to pass the blood-brain barrier) that transform molecules into feature vectors (e.g. \ce{CCO} could translate to a vector $[2,46.07,1,1]$ where each number represents a feature of the molecule, in this example }%DIFDELCMD < [%%%
\DIFdel{\# \ce{C}, MW, \# \ce{H}-bond donors, \# \ce{H}-bond acceptors}%DIFDELCMD < ]%%%
\DIFdel{) for use with a random forest model, which they consider }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{interpretable}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
This approach outperformed specialized }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{sota} %%%
\DIFdel{models across $58$ benchmark tasks, while providing interpretable reasoning about prediction logic }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{that extract interpretable rules }\autocite{zheng2025large} \DIFaddend (see \Cref{tab:property_prediction_models}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{for properties predicted by this model).
However, its reliance on rule extraction may limit its ability to capture complex, non-linear relationships that specialized deep learning models can identify.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{).
}\DIFaddend 



\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{as Feature Extractors}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend Another emerging application of \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{llm}\DIFadd{-based }\glspl{gpm} \DIFaddend is their use as \enquote{feature extractors}, where they generate textual or embedded representations of molecules or materials. For instance, in materials science, \textcite{aneesh2025semantic} employed \glspl{llm} to generate text embeddings of perovskite solar cell compositions. 
These embeddings were subsequently used to train a \gls{gnn} for predicting power conversion efficiency, demonstrating the potential of \glspl{llm} to enhance feature representation in materials informatics. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Similarly, in the molecular domain, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{srinivas2024crossmodal} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
used zero-shot }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{prompting (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{box: cot_prompting} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
for prompt examples) to generate detailed textual descriptions of molecular functional groups, which are used to train a small }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{lm}%%%
\DIFdel{. This }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{lm} %%%
\DIFdel{is used to compute text-level embeddings of molecules. Simultaneously, they generate molecular graph-level embeddings from }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{string molecular graph inputs. They finally integrate the graph and text-level embeddings to produce a semantically enriched embedding.
}%DIFDELCMD < \begin{promptbox}\label{box: cot_prompting}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{promptbox}[label={box: cot_prompting}]
    \DIFaddend \textbf{\gls{cot} Prompting}\autocite{srinivas2024crossmodal}\\
    Prompt 1: What is the molecular structure of this chemical \gls{smiles} string? Could you describe its atoms, bonds, functional groups, and overall arrangement? \\
    Prompt 2: What are the physical properties of this molecule, such as its boiling point and melting point?\\
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{...}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \dots\DIFaddend \\
    Prompt 14: Are there any environmental impacts associated with the production, use, or disposal of this molecule?
\end{promptbox}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In a different implementation of fine-tuning, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{balaji2023gptmolberta} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
used \modelname{ChatGPT} to generate text descriptions of molecules that were then }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Similarly, in the molecular domain, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{srinivas2024crossmodal} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
used zero-shot }\gls{llm} \DIFadd{prompting (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{box: cot_prompting} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
for prompt examples) to generate detailed textual descriptions of molecular functional groups, which are }\DIFaddend used to train a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{RoBERTa} (125M) model for property prediction, showing how }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{small }\DIFaddend \gls{lm}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{-generated representations can access latent spaces that }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. This }\gls{lm} \DIFadd{is used to compute text-level embeddings of molecules. Simultaneously, they generate molecular graph-level embeddings from }\DIFaddend \gls{smiles} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{strings alone might not capture. Similarly, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{li2024unveiling} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
introduced the \modelname{MoleX} framework, which fine-tunes \modelname{ChemBERTa-2}}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{ahmad2022chemberta} %%%
\DIFdel{on Group }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{selfies} \autocite{cheng2023group} %%%
\DIFdel{(a functional group-based molecular representation) to then extract a single }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{-derived embedding of moleculesthat captures the chemical semantics at the functional group level. This allowed them to determine which functional groups or fragments contribute to molecular properties, which in turn can be converted into reliable explanations of said properties. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{string molecular graph inputs. They finally integrate the graph and text-level embeddings to produce a semantically enriched embedding.
}\DIFaddend 


\subsubsection{Fine-Tuning}\label{sec:prediction_FT}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[htb] 
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \begin{figure}[H] 
    \DIFaddendFL \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/property_gptchem.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Fine-tuned \modelname{GPT-3} for predicting solid-solution formation in high-entropy alloys} Performance comparison of different \gls{ml} approaches as a function of the number of training points. Results are shown for \modelname{Automatminer} (blue), \modelname{CrabNet} transformer (orange),  fine-tuned \modelname{GPT-3} (red), with error bars showing standard error of the mean. The non-Google test set shows the fine-tuned \modelname{GPT-3} model tested on compounds without an exact Google search match (dark red). The dashed line shows performance using random forest \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{(RF)}\DIFaddendFL . \modelname{GPT-3} achieves comparable accuracy to traditional approaches with \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{significantly }\DIFdelendFL fewer training examples. Data adapted from \textcite{jablonka2024leveraging}}
    \label{fig:gptchem}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{\gls{lift}} \textcite{dinh2022lift} showed that reformulating regression and classification as \gls{qa} tasks enables the use of \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{an }\DIFaddend unmodified model architecture while improving performance (see \Cref{sec:fine-tuning} for a deeper discussion of \gls{lift}). 
In recognizing the scarcity of experimental data and acknowledging the persistence of this limitation, \textcite{jablonka2024leveraging} designed a \gls{lift}-based framework using \modelname{GPT-3} fine-tuned on task-specific small datasets (see \Cref{tab:property_prediction_models}). 
They \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{seminally }\DIFdelend demonstrated that fine-tuned \modelname{GPT-3} can match or surpass specialized \gls{ml} models in various chemistry tasks \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. A key finding was fine-tuned \modelname{GPT-3}'s ability to generalize beyond training data. 
When tested on compounds absent from Google Search (and likely its training data), it performed well, proving that it was not simply recalling memorized information (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:gptchem}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(as shown in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:gptchem}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

In a follow-up to \textcite{jablonka2024leveraging}'s work, \textcite{vanherck2025assessment} systematically evaluated this approach across 22 diverse real-world chemistry case studies using three open-source models. They demonstrate that fine-tuned \glspl{llm} can \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{effectively }\DIFdelend predict various material properties. For example, they achieved $96\%$ accuracy in predicting the adhesive free-energy of polymers, outperforming traditional \gls{ml} methods like random forest ($90\%$ accuracy). \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{When predicting properties of monomers using }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{notation, the fine-tuned models reached average accuracies of $84\%$ across four different properties. Particularly notable was the ability of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{can also }\DIFaddend work with non-standard inputs, like \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{for predicting }\DIFaddend protein phase separation\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{study they did}\DIFdelend , where raw protein sequences could be directly input without pre-processing and achieve $95\%$ prediction accuracy. At the same time, when training datasets were very small (15 data points), the predictive accuracy of all fine-tuned models was lower than the random baseline (e.g.\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend MOF synthesis). These case studies preliminarily demonstrate that these models can achieve predictive performance with some small datasets, work with various chemical representations (\gls{smiles}, \gls{mof}id, and \gls{iupac} names), and can outperform traditional \gls{ml} approaches for some material property prediction tasks.

In the materials domain, \modelname{LLMprop} fine-tunes \modelname{T5}\autocite{raffel2020exploring} to predict crystalline material properties from text descriptions generated by \modelname{Robocrystallographer}\autocite{ganose2019robocrystallographer}. By discarding \modelname{T5}’s decoder and adding task-specific prediction heads, the approach reduces computational overhead while leveraging the model’s ability to process structured crystal descriptions. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The method demonstrates that natural language representations can effectively capture key material features, offering an alternative to traditional graph-based models like }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gnn}%%%
\DIFdel{.
}\DIFdelend 

Fine-tuning has \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{been used to adapt }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{also been applied to non-}\gls{llm} \DIFadd{architectures, specifically to }\DIFaddend \glspl{ssm} like Mamba (see \Cref{sec:example_architectures}). By pre-training on 91 million molecules, the Mamba-based model \modelname{$\text{O}_{SMI}-{\text{SSM}-}336\textit{M}$} outperformed transformer methods (\modelname{Yield-BERT}\autocite{krzyzanowski2025exploring}) in reaction yield prediction (e.g., Buchwald-Hartwig cross-coupling) and achieved competitive results in molecular property prediction benchmarks.\autocite{soares2025mamba-based} 

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Foundational }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gnn} %%%
\DIFdel{and }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{mlip}%%%
} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The fine-tuning approach has been applied to }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{foundational \glspl{gnn}} \autocite{sypetkowski2024scalability, shoghi2023molecules} %%%
\DIFdel{and }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{mlip}%%%
\DIFdel{, approaches distinct from }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{. For example, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{shoghi2023molecules, sypetkowski2024scalability} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
show }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{sota} %%%
\DIFdel{performance on property prediction tasks.
}%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Foundational} \glspl{mlip} %%%
\DIFdel{pre-trained on large datasets encompassing many chemical elements can be fine-tuned for specific downstream tasks }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{batatia2022mace}%%%
\DIFdel{, such as calculating sublimation enthalpies of molecular crystal polymorphs }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{kaur2025data}%%%
\DIFdel{.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{Limitations}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{One central challenge is finding balance in datasets. In practical applications, researchers often have many more examples of poor-performing materials than optimal ones, resulting in unbalanced datasets that can diminish model performance. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{vanherck2025assessment} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
point out that in the catalyzed cleavage reaction study, only $3.8\%$ of catalysts were labeled as }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{good}%%%
\DIFdel{, forcing researchers to reduce their training set significantly to maintain balance. They also note that }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{struggle with highly complex or noisy datasets, as seen in their study of catalytic isomerization, where even after hyperparameter optimization, the models failed to achieve meaningful predictive power due to the high noise in the experimental data and limited sample size. Finally, they note that although }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{can work with different chemical representations, the choice of representation significantly impacts performance. For example, when predicting polymerization rates, models using }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{notation significantly outperformed those using }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{iupac} %%%
\DIFdel{names, indicating that representation selection remains an important consideration.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Fine-tuning effectively adapts }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{to specialized chemistry tasks, but its dependence on static datasets hinders adaptability to new or evolving knowledge. }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{rag}%%%
\DIFdel{, whose fundamentals are described in detail in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:rag}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, overcomes these limitations by dynamically integrating external data sources, enabling more flexible and up-to-date reasoning.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{Agents}

Caldas Ramos et al.\ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{introduce }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{introduced }\DIFaddend \modelname{MAPI-LLM}, a framework that processes natural-language queries about material properties using an \gls{llm} to decide which of the available tools\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend such as the Materials Project \gls{api}, the Reaction-Network package, or Google Search\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend to use to generate a response. \autocite{Jablonka2023} \modelname{MAPI-LLM} employs a \gls{react} prompt (see \Cref{sec:arch_agents} to read more about \gls{react}), to convert prompts such as  \textit{\enquote{Is $Fe_2O_3$ magnetic?}} or \textit{\enquote{What is the band gap of Mg(Fe3O3)2?}} into queries for Materials Project \gls{api}. 
The system processes multi-step prompts through logical reasoning, for example, when asked \textit{\enquote{If Mn2FeO3 is not metallic, what is its band gap?}}, the \gls{llm} system creates a two-step workflow to first verify metallicity before retrieving the band gap.

Building on this foundation of agent-based materials querying, \textcite{chiang2024llamp} advanced the approach with \modelname{LLaMP}, a framework that employs \enquote{hierarchical} \gls{react} agents to interact with computational and experimental data. This \enquote{hierarchical} framework employs a supervisor-assistant agent architecture where a complex problem is broken down and tasks are delegated to domain-specific agents.   
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{LLaMP} addresses the challenge of hallucinations more effectively than standard }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{approaches by grounding responses in retrieved materials databases, retrieving materials data (e.g., crystal structures, elastic tensors) while counteracting systematic }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{biases in property predictions. These biases include the tendency for }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{to overestimate certain properties like bulk moduli and to exhibit errors in bandgap predictions based on compositional patterns learned during training rather than physical principles.
}\DIFdelend 


\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Core }\DIFdelend Limitations}\label{sec:property_core_limits}

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[htb]
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \begin{figure}[H]
    \DIFaddendFL \centering
    \includegraphics{figures/property_mattext.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Normalized error distributions for materials property prediction models across different architectures}. Each point represents the normalized error of a model on a specific property prediction task. Normalization was achieved with min/max values of each dataset to produce a range of errors between 0 and 1. The first column (blue) shows \gls{gnn} based models, the second column (red) displays \gls{llm} approaches, and the third column (orange) represents other baseline methods and \gls{sota} models\DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, }\DIFaddendFL including \modelname{CrabNet}. \autocite{Wang_2021} Lower values indicate better predictive performance. Data adapted from \textcite{alampara2024mattext}}
    \label{fig:property_limitations}
\end{figure}

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \noindent %%%
\DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{alampara2024mattext} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
introduced \modelname{MatText}, a framework for evaluating }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{lm} %%%
\DIFdel{ability to predict properties of materials using text-based representations. Their findings indicate that current }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{(including pre-trained \modelname{BERT} and fine-tuned \modelname{LLaMA-3-8B}) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A significant challenge for }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{in chemistry lies in managing dataset limitations and selecting appropriate chemical representations. Practical applications often suffer from highly unbalanced datasets, where examples of optimal materials are vastly outnumbered by poor-performing ones, forcing difficult compromises that can diminish model performance }\autocite{vanherck2025assessment}\DIFadd{. The choice of how a material is represented (}\gls{smiles} \DIFadd{notation versus }\gls{iupac}\DIFadd{) also critically impacts performance, indicating that data preprocessing remains a crucial consideration.
}

\DIFadd{Beyond data issues, architectural constraints present another barrier as illustrated in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:property_limitations}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{alampara2024mattext} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
reveal that while }\glspl{llm} \DIFaddend are effective for tasks relying \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{purely }\DIFdelend on compositional information\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(e.g., element types and local bonding patterns), but struggle to leverage geometric or positional information }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, they struggle to interpret geometric or spatial data when it is }\DIFaddend encoded in text\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, as reflected in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:property_limitations}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. This observation suggests that }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. This suggests a fundamental limitation of }\DIFaddend transformer-based architectures \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{may be fundamentally limited to applications where spatial understanding is not required. Their experiments with data scaling and text representations reveal that increasing pre-training data or adding geometric details fails to improve downstream property prediction, challenging }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{for applications requiring spatial reasoning. Consequently, }\DIFaddend the conventional assumption that \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{larger models and datasets universally enhance performance . }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{performance can be universally improved by scaling up model size or pre-training data is challenged }\DIFaddend \autocite{frey2023neural}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Notably, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{frey2023neural} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
demonstrated power-law scaling in chemical }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{, but \modelname{MatText}'s results imply that such scaling }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Such scaling }\DIFaddend may not overcome \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{architectural biases against geometric reasoning in materials tasks.
}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{gruver2024promises}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the inherent bias against geometric understanding.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Encoding 3D Structure}}\DIFadd{: Developing methods to effectively represent and integrate geometric, spatial, and structural information to overcome their inherent bias toward compositional data.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Dynamic Knowledge Integration}}\DIFadd{: Move beyond static fine-tuning to establish a reliable, real-time framework that can incorporate new, evolving scientific findings without catastrophic forgetting.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Fundamental Architectural Shifts}}\DIFadd{: Exploring whether many of the existing }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{architectures are sufficient or if new, specialized ones are needed to capture complex relationships in molecular and materials science.
}\end{itemize}


\DIFaddend \subsection{Molecular and Material Generation} \label{sec:mol_generation}

\begin{figure}[htbp!]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure21.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Pipeline for molecular and materials generation}\DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{. }\DIFaddendFL The workflow begins with input structures represented in various formats, which are used to train \gls{ml} models to generate novel molecular and material structures. The generated structures should undergo a feedback loop through validation processes before being applied in the real world. Blue boxes indicate well-established areas of the pipeline with mature methodologies, while the red box represents critical bottlenecks. \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Currently, the largest gaps in the process are in the representation and validation steps.}\DIFaddendFL }
    \label{fig:generation}
\end{figure}

\noindent Early work in molecular and materials generation relied heavily on unconditional generation, where models produce novel structures without explicit guidance\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, relying solely on patterns learned from training data. For example, latent space sampling in autoencoders, where random vectors are decoded into new structures.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{yoshikai2024novel} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. }\DIFaddend These methods excel at exploring chemical space broadly but lack \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{fine-grained control. 
This limitation underscores the need for conditional generation, using }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{specific control. 
Conditional generation, in contrast, uses }\DIFaddend explicit prompts or constraints (e.g., property targets, structural fragments) \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, }\DIFdelend to steer \glspl{gpm} toward meaningful molecule or material designs. 
Beyond the generation step, as \Cref{fig:generation} shows, critical bottlenecks persist in synthesizability and physical consistency at the validation stage.

\subsubsection{Generation}\label{sec:generation}

\paragraph{Prompting}
While zero-shot and few-shot prompting strategies \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{demonstrate promising flexibility for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{offer a flexible approach to }\DIFaddend molecule generation, benchmark studies \autocite{guo2023large} reveal \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{significant limitations that restrict their practical utility. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{guo2023large} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
exposed fundamental gaps in }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{a significant performance gap compared to specialized models. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{guo2023large} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
systematically evaluated }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{' molecular design capabilities through a systematic evaluation. \modelname{GPT-4} was reported to produce chemically valid }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{$89\%$ of the time but achieving less than $20\%$ accuracy in matching the target specifications. 
This result is far below specialized modelslike \modelname{MolT5}}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{edwards2022translation}%%%
\DIFdel{. They conclude that this performance gap stems from }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{' inadequate understanding of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{syntax and structure-property relationships. 
Subsequent work by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{bhattacharya2024large} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
explored whether systematic prompt engineering could overcome these limitations, demonstrating that these prompts could guide \modelname{Claude 3 Opus} to generate chemically }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{like \modelname{GPT-4}, finding that while they could generate syntactically }\DIFaddend valid molecules (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$97\%$ syntactic validity)with controlled modifications, including fine-grained structural changes (median Tanimoto similarity $0.67$--$0.69$) and predictable electronic property shifts (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\SIrange{0.14}{0.27}{eV} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}%DIFDELCMD < \gls{homo} %%%
\DIFdel{energy changes). Hybrid approaches like \modelname{FrontierX} extend this method with knowledge-augmented prompting, where }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$89\%$ validity), their accuracy in meeting specific property targets was low ($< 20\%$). These performance gaps highlight the lack of chemical structure-property relationships possessed by }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{generate both molecule predictions and explanations that are used to fine-tune smaller }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{lm}%%%
\DIFdel{, with all resulting embeddings ultimately combined via hierarchical attention mechanisms to produce the final }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{representation}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{srinivas2024crossing}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
It showed improved accuracy over pure prompting strategies but sacrificed the generalizability that makes }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{attractive, as the model requires re-training for each new molecular domain}\DIFdelend .

\paragraph{Fine-Tuning} To overcome the limitations of prompting, fine-tuning has been adopted in molecular and materials generation, much like its use in property prediction with \gls{lift}-based frameworks (see \Cref{sec:fine-tuning} for a deeper explanation of \gls{lift} and \Cref{sec:prediction_FT} for a discussion of \gls{lift} applied to property prediction tasks). 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{yu2024llasmol} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
demonstrated that systematic fine-tuning in various chemical tasks including molecule generation from captions can improve performance while remaining parameter-efficient, using only $0.58\%$ of trainable parameters via }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{lora}%%%
\DIFdel{.
}\DIFdelend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The molecule-caption translation task (\modelname{Mol2Cap}), which involves generating textual descriptions from molecular representations and vice versa (Cap2Mol), has become a standard benchmark for evaluating }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{for molecule generation. }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{edwards2022translation} %%%
\DIFdel{Under the }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Mol2Cap}%%%
\DIFdel{/}%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Cap2Mol} %%%
\DIFdel{task paradigm, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icma} %%%
\DIFdel{avoids domain-specific pre-training by combining }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A representative example is }\gls{icma}\DIFadd{, which combines }\DIFaddend retrieval-augmented in-context learning with fine-tuning \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{on }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icl} %%%
\DIFdel{examples .}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{li2025large} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{On the ChEBI-20}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{edwards2021text2mol} %%%
\DIFdel{and PubChem324k}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{liu2023molca} %%%
\DIFdel{datasets}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \autocite{li2025large}\DIFadd{. This method avoids extensive domain-specific pre-training by retrieving relevant examples to guide the model. On standard benchmarks}\DIFaddend , \gls{icma} nearly \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{doubles baseline performance , with }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{icma} %%%
\DIFdel{powered by\modelname{Mistral-7B} achieving a 0.581 }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{bleu} %%%

%DIFDELCMD < %%%

Similarly, \modelname{MolReFlect} enhances fine-grained alignment through a teacher-student framework, where a larger }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{(e.g., \modelname{GPT-4}) extracts substructure-aware captions to guide a smaller model (\modelname{Mistral-7B}), improving \modelname{Cap2Mol} accuracy while reducing hallucinations.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{li2024molreflect} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%

}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Fine-tuned }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{lm} %%%

\paragraph{Reinforcement Learning and Preference Optimization}

Translating \gls{gpm} generated outputs to the real world requires designing molecules and materials with specific target properties. \gls{rl} and preference optimization techniques\autocite{lee2024fine-tuning} have emerged as \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{powerful }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{potential }\DIFaddend solutions for this challenge. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{For instance, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{jang2025can} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
combined }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{sft} %%%
\DIFdel{and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{\modelname{CrystalFormer-RL} uses }\DIFaddend \gls{rl} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{using }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ppo} %%%
\DIFdel{to generate diverse molecular sequences auto-regressively. 
This approach excels in exploring a broad chemical space, but incurs high computational costs due to its reliance on iterative, sequence-based generation. 
In contrast, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{cavanagh2024smileyllama} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
employed }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{dpo} %%%
\DIFdel{with }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{sft} %%%
\DIFdel{to fine-tune }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{for molecular design, leveraging }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{representations to optimize drug-like properties }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{fine-tuning to optimize \modelname{CrystalFormer}}\autocite{cao2024space}\DIFadd{, a transformer-based crystal generator, with rewards from discriminative models }\DIFaddend (e.g., \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{hydrogen bond donors/acceptors and LogP).
While }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{dpo} %%%
\DIFdel{reduces computational overhead in comparison to }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ppo}%%%
\DIFdel{, it trades off molecular diversity, a key strength of the work by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{jang2025can}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, due to the inherent constraints of preference-based }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{property predictors)}\autocite{cao2025crystalformer-rl}\DIFadd{. Here, }\gls{rl} \DIFaddend fine-tuning \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is shown to outperform supervised fine-tuning, enhancing both novel material discovery and retrieval of high-performing candidates from the pre-training dataset}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Beyond these methods, }\DIFdelend \gls{era} introduces a different optimization paradigm. \autocite{chennakesavalu2025aligning} Unlike \gls{ppo} or \gls{dpo}, \gls{era} uses gradient-based objectives to guide word-by-word generation with explicit reward functions, converging to a physics-inspired probability distribution that allows fine control over the generation process.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In single-property optimization tasks, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{era} %%%
\DIFdel{successfully aligned molecular transformers to generate compounds with targeted chemical properties (QED, LogP, ring count, molar refractivity) while maintaining $59-84\%$ chemical validity without regularization. For multi-objective optimization, it achieved precise control over property trade-offs using weighted energy functions.
}\DIFdelend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{calanzone2025mol-moe} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
also address the challenge of multi-objective molecular generation with \modelname{MOL-MOE}, a }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{moe} %%%
\DIFdel{framework (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:arch-moes} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
to learn more about }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{moe} %%%
\DIFdel{architectures). \modelname{MOL-MOE} dynamically combines property-specific expert models at test time using preference-guided routers toward drug-relevant molecular properties enabling flexible steering across multiple objectives without re-training. Compared to alternatives like \modelname{MORLHF}}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{zhou2024one-preference-fits-all}%%%
\DIFdel{, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{sft} %%%
\DIFdel{with rewards-in-context, and simple model merging such as Rewarded Soups}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{rame2023rewarded}%%%
\DIFdel{), \modelname{MOL-MOE} achieves superior performance in both property optimization and steerability---particularly in out-of-distribution scenarios where other methods struggle. 
}%DIFDELCMD <
