\DIFdel{\modelname{CrystalFormer-RL} uses }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{rl} %%%
\DIFdel{fine-tuning to optimize \modelname{CrystalFormer}}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{cao2024space}%%%
\DIFdel{, a transformer-based crystal generator, with rewards from discriminative models (e.g., property predictors)}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{cao2025crystalformer-rl}%%%
\DIFdel{. }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{rl} %%%
\DIFdel{improves stability (lower energy above convex hull) and enables property-guided generation (e.g., high dielectric constant + band gap). Here, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{rl} %%%
\DIFdel{fine-tuning is shown to outperform supervised fine-tuning, enhancing both novel material discovery and retrieval of high-performing candidates from the pre-training dataset.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \paragraph{Agents} Agent-based frameworks leveraging \glspl{llm},  \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{deeply }\DIFdelend explained in \Cref{sec:agents}, have emerged as approaches for autonomous molecular and materials generation, demonstrating capabilities that extend beyond simple prompting or fine-tuning by incorporating iterative feedback loops, tool integration, and human-\gls{ai} collaboration. 
The \modelname{dZiner} framework \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{implements }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{exemplifies }\DIFaddend this approach for the inverse design of materials, where agents input initial \gls{smiles} strings with optimization task descriptions and generate validated candidate molecules by retrieving domain knowledge from the literature.\autocite{ansari2024dziner} 
It also uses domain-expert surrogate models to evaluate the required property in the new molecule/material. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{These surrogate models are highly customizable to the desired property and give the user the option to train their own }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ml} %%%
\DIFdel{model or using an existing }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{sota} %%%
\DIFdel{model. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{ansari2024dziner} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
demonstrated \modelname{dZiner}'s capabilities in generating surfactants for critical micelle concentration reduction, WDR5 inhibitors, and optimizing }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{mof} %%%
\DIFdel{organic linkers for \ce{CO2} adsorption. The \modelname{CLADD} framework adopts a }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{rag}%%%
\DIFdel{-enhanced multi-agent approach where specialized teams including }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Planning}%%%
\DIFdel{, }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Knowledge Graph}%%%
\DIFdel{, and }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Molecular Understanding} %%%
\DIFdel{collaborate to dynamically retrieve and integrate external biochemical knowledge for drug discovery tasks without requiring domain-specific fine-tuning.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{lee2025rag-enhanced}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend 

\subsubsection{Validation}
\paragraph{General validation} The most fundamental validation approaches use cheminformatics tools like \modelname{RDKit} to verify molecular validity. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{RDKit} provides robust tools for validating molecules through its ability to parse and sanitize molecules from }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{strings. 
If a step in the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{smiles} %%%
\DIFdel{to structure conversion process fails, then the molecule is considered invalid. }\DIFdelend More sophisticated validation involves quantum mechanical calculations to compute molecular properties such as formation energies\autocite{kingsbury2022flexible}. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{These computationally expensive operations provide deeper insights into whether generated structures are viable. Models are also evaluated for their ability to generate unique molecules by calculating the proportion of unique molecules in generated sets, often using molecular fingerprints or structural descriptors. 
}\DIFdelend 

The gold standard for validation is experimental synthesis, but significant gaps exist between computational generation and laboratory realization.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Preliminarily, metrics like Tanimoto similarity and Fréchet ChemNet distance }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{preuer2018frechet} %%%
\DIFdel{quantify structural resemblance, which can indicate synthetic feasibility when training data consists of known compounds. }\DIFdelend Retrosynthesis prediction algorithms attempt to bridge this gap by evaluating synthetic accessibility and proposing potential synthesis routes (see \Cref{sec:retrosynthesis}). 
However, these methods still face limitations in accurately predicting real-world synthesizability \autocite{zunger2019beware}.

\paragraph{Conditional Generation Validation} Beyond establishing the general validity of generated molecules, evaluation methods can assess both their novelty relative to training data and their ability to meet specific design goals. For inverse design tasks, such as optimizing binding affinity or solubility, the \textit{de novo} molecule generation benchmark GuacaMol differentiates between \textit{distribution-learning} (e.g., generating diverse, valid molecules) and \textit{goal-directed} optimization (e.g., rediscovering known drugs or meeting multi-objective constraints) \autocite{brown2019guacamol}. 
In the materials paradigm, frameworks such as \modelname{MatBench Discovery} evaluate analogous challenges such as stability, electronic properties, and synthesizability, but adapt metrics to periodic systems, such as energy above hull or band gap prediction accuracy\autocite{riebesell2025framework}. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Recently, they introduced the }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{discovery acceleration factor}%%%
\DIFdel{, which quantifies how effective a model is at finding stable structures relative to a random baseline.
}\DIFdelend 

\DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Limitations}}
\DIFadd{Current generative pipelines for molecules and materials, as illustrated in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:generation}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, face significant bottlenecks that limit their immediate real-world application. A primary limitation is the performance gap in conditional generation; while methods like fine-tuning and reinforcement learning have improved control, }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{often lack the inherent chemical understanding to accurately meet specific property targets. Furthermore, the validation stage remains a critical challenge. Even when a structure is computationally valid, assessing its synthesizability and physical consistency relies heavily on approximations, with a significant gap remaining between in-silico prediction and experimental realization.
}

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Robust Conditional Generation}} \DIFadd{Developing models that can reliably generate novel, diverse structures that satisfy complex, multi-objective constraints (e.g., high stability, specific electronic properties, and synthesizability) without over-relying on retrieved training data examples.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Bridging the Synthesis Gap}} \DIFadd{Creating new validation metrics that better predict the synthetic feasibility of generated materials and molecules, moving beyond structural similarity toward realistic pathway prediction.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Unified Multi-Scale Generation}} \DIFadd{Developing frameworks that are capable of simultaneously modeling different representations (e.g., text, graphs, 3D structures) and scales (e.g., from molecules to crystalline materials) within a single generative process
 }\end{itemize}

 
\DIFaddend \subsection{Retrosynthesis}\label{sec:retrosynthesis}

The practical utility of \glspl{gpm} for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{generating }\DIFdelend molecules and materials \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{remains limited by a persistent gap in their }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{design is constrained by uncertainty in }\DIFaddend synthetic feasibility. 
Early work \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{schwaller2021mapping} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
laid important groundwork by demonstrating how }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{showed that }\DIFaddend attention-based \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{neural networks can learn meaningful representations of chemical reactions}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{models learn meaningful reaction representations}\DIFaddend , enabling accurate \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reaction outcome }\DIFaddend classification and prediction \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{of reaction outcomes.
Their model, trained on millions of reactions from patent and literature data, showed that learned reaction embeddings were capable of capturing nuanced chemical relationships. 
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \autocite{schwaller2021mapping}\DIFadd{.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Recent efforts have built on thisfoundation by integrating synthesizability directly into molecular and materials generation pipelines that leverage both domain-specific }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Building on this, generation pipelines increasingly integrate synthesizability and retrosynthetic guidance via domain }\DIFaddend tools and \glspl{gpm} \DIFaddbegin \autocite{liu2024multimodal}\DIFaddend . 
For example, \textcite{sun2025synllama} adapted \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{Llama-3.1-8B} and \modelname{Llama-3.2-1B} to predict retrosynthetic pathways and identify commercially available }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{open }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{to propose retrosynthetic routes and identify purchasable }\DIFaddend building blocks for experimentally validated SARS-CoV-2 Mpro inhibitors.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Similarly, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{liu2024multimodal} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
introduced a multimodal framework that combines reaction databases with chemical intuition encoded in }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm}%%%
\DIFdel{, improving the prioritization of high-yield, low-cost synthetic routes.
}\DIFdelend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{More recent work has explored how fully fine-tuned }\DIFdelend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{can serve as comprehensive }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{are also being fine-tuned as }\DIFaddend chemistry assistants for experimental guidance.
\textcite{zhang2025large} used a two-stage \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{training processto first develop \modelname{Chemma-SFT} by }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{process, supervised }\DIFaddend fine-tuning \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{LLaMA-2-7B} on 1.28 million chemical reaction question-answer pairs about reaction prediction, single-step retrosynthesis, and reaction condition generation tasks. 
In the second stage of training, they developed \modelname{Chemma-RM} using }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{with reaction and retrosynthesis }\gls{qa} \DIFadd{followed by }\DIFaddend \gls{rlhf}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and applied it to optimize the experimental reaction conditions. \modelname{Chemma} successfully optimized an unreported Suzuki-Miyaura }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{) to optimize reaction conditions, achieving an unreported Suzuki–Miyaura }\DIFaddend cross-coupling \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{reaction within only }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in }\DIFaddend 15 \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{experimental }\DIFdelend runs.

Predictive retrosynthesis has also extended to the inorganic domain. \textcite{kim2024large} demonstrated that fine-tuned \modelname{GPT-3.5}\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend and \modelname{GPT-4} can predict both the synthesizability of inorganic compounds from their chemical formulas and select appropriate precursors for synthesis, achieving performance comparable to specialized \gls{ml} models with minimal development time and cost. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In a follow-up work, they extended this approach to structure-based predictions of inorganic crystal polymorphs, where }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{provided human-readable explanations for their synthesizability assessments}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{kim2025explainable}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
Notably, their structure-aware models correctly identified twelve hypothetical compounds as non-synthesizable despite their thermodynamic stability, perfectly matching experimental outcomes where synthesis attempts failed.
}\DIFdelend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Beyond retrosynthetic prediction, }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{have also been deployed as reasoning engines for autonomous design. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Toward autonomy, }\DIFaddend \textcite{bran2024augmenting} developed \modelname{ChemCrow}, an \gls{llm}-based system that autonomously plans and executes the synthesis of novel compounds by integrating specialized tools like a retrosynthesis planner (see \Cref{sec:planning} to read more about this capability of \modelname{ChemCrow} and its limitations) and reaction predictors. 
This approach mirrors the iterative experimental design cycle employed by human chemists, but is equipped with the scalability of automation. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Notably, systems like \modelname{ChemCrow} rely on high-quality reaction data to ground their reasoningin empirically viable chemistry, which}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Limitations}}

\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{inherit the fundamental constraints associated with domain-specific models for retrosynthesis, which primarily concern data and evaluation methodologies. The prevailing reliance on patent-derived data introduces a significant bias, as these datasets are dominated by a limited set of reaction types and predominantly feature single-step examples. Furthermore, the absence of negative results and the frequent omission of experimental conditions can mislead model training }\autocite{Lee2024noise, Voinarovska2023when, Saebi2023onthe, Maloney2023negative, Tadanki2025dissecting}\DIFadd{. The evaluation paradigms suffer from a parallel shortcoming, as they are largely designed for single-step routes and fail to adequately capture the complexities of real-world}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{depending on the design space, could be a limitation.
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{multi-step case studies }\autocite{TorrenPeraire2024models, maziarz2025re, liu2024evaluating}\DIFadd{.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A further significant challenge is data leakage. The vast scale of data used for training these models often leads to saturated benchmarks, where high performance may reflect memorization rather than genuine generalization.
}

\DIFadd{Finally, while standalone }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{shows limited efficacy for multi-step retrosynthesis, its integration into agentic frameworks introduces a distinct set of limitations. Current evaluations are poorly suited to assess the critical components of such systems, such as planning, reasoning, and tool-calling capabilities. The focus remains predominantly on final answer correctness, which impedes large-scale optimization and correction of agents without extensive human oversight, typically resulting in the use of a fragile and error-prone LLM-as-Judge. 
}

\DIFadd{These evaluation gaps, combined with the inherent limitations in long-term planning and chemical understanding of the underlying }\glspl{llm}\DIFadd{, present substantial obstacles to the development and practical application of agentic systems for this retrosynthetic task.
}


\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Data Quality and Bias}}\DIFadd{: To build more robust and generalizable models, the field must integrate higher-quality data from peer-reviewed scientific literature. This requires the development of standardized extraction schemas and data cleaning protocols to create a balanced and reliable training corpus }\autocite{rios2025llm, mehr2020universal}\DIFadd{.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Robust and Meaningful Evaluation}}\DIFadd{: There is a critical need for evaluation methodologies that prevent data leakage and test genuine model understanding. Benchmarks must be designed to go beyond memorization, instead probing a model's capacity for chemical reasoning, generalization to novel structures, and robust problem-solving.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Benchmarking Complex Planning}}\DIFadd{: Truly assessing a }\gls{gpm}\DIFadd{'s utility requires benchmarks that mirror real-world complexity. This involves evaluating performance in open-ended retrosynthetic planning and developing frameworks that assess a model's sequential decision-making.
}\end{itemize}

\DIFaddend \subsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{LLMs }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{GPMs }\DIFaddend as Optimizers} \label{sec:llm-optimizers}

\begin{figure}[htb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/rescaled_figures/chemrev_figure22.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Overview of the iterative optimization loop that mirrors the structure of the optimization section}. The blue boxes contain the different roles that the \glspl{llm} play in the loop, and which are described in the main text. References in which the use of \glspl{llm} for that step are detailed inside the small boxes inside each of the components of the loop. The example shown is about obtaining molecules with high \texttt{logP}.}
    \label{fig:optimization}
\end{figure}

Discovering novel compounds and reactions in chemistry and materials science has long relied on iterative trial-and-error processes rooted in existing domain knowledge \autocite{Taylor2023brief}. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{While, as explained }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{As noted }\DIFaddend in \Cref{sec:retrosynthesis}, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{those methods are used to accelerate this process, optimization methods help improve conditions , binding affinity, etc. 
But these approaches are }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{these methods accelerate discovery, and optimization targets variables such as conditions or binding affinity. 
Nevertheless, the overall process remains }\DIFaddend slow and labor-intensive.
Traditional data-driven methods \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{aimed }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{aim }\DIFaddend to address these limitations by combining predictive \gls{ml} models with optimization frameworks such as \gls{bo} or \glspl{ea}. 
These frameworks balance exploration of uncharted regions in chemical space with exploitation of known high-performing regions \autocite{Li2024sequential, Hse2021gryffin, Shields2021bayesian, Griffiths2020constrained, RajabiKochi2025adaptive}.

Recent advances in \glspl{llm} have \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{unlocked potential for addressing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{been explored for targeting }\DIFaddend optimization challenges in chemistry and related domains \autocite{fernando2023promptbreeder0, yang2023large, chen2024instruct}. 
A \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{key strength of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{commonly referenced advantage is that }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{lies in their capacity to frame optimization tasks through }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{may process optimization tasks posed in }\DIFaddend natural language, which \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{enhances }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{may facilitate }\DIFaddend knowledge incorporation, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{improves candidate comparisons, and increases interpretability . 
This aligns well }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{candidate comparison, and interpretability in some settings.
This can align }\DIFaddend with chemical problem-solving, where complex phenomena, such as reaction pathways or material behaviors, are often poorly captured by standard nomenclature; however, they can still be intuitively explained through natural language. 
Moreover, \glspl{gpm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{' general capabilities provide flexibility beyond classical methods, which have to be trained from scratch if the optimization problem or any of its variables changes.
By encoding }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{can offer flexibility when problem definitions change, whereas many classical models require retraining.
Encoding }\DIFaddend domain-specific knowledge---including reaction rules, thermodynamic principles, and structure-property relationships---into structured prompts \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{may allow }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{can synergize }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to complement domain }\DIFaddend expertise with their ability to navigate complex chemical optimization problems.

Current \gls{llm} applications in chemistry optimization vary in scope and methodology. 
Many studies integrate \glspl{llm} into \gls{bo} frameworks, where models guide experimental design by predicting promising candidates \autocite{rankovic2023bochemian}. Others employ \glspl{ga} or hybrid strategies that combine \gls{llm}-generated hypotheses with computational screening \autocite{cisse2025language0based}.  

\subsubsection{LLMs as Surrogate Models}

A prominent \gls{llm}-driven strategy positions these models as surrogate models within optimization loops \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. Typically }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \autocite{yu2025collaborative}\DIFadd{. 
Surrogate models---often }\DIFaddend implemented as \gls{gpr}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, surrogate models learn }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{---learn }\DIFaddend from prior data to approximate costly feature-outcome landscapes, which are often computationally \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{expensive }\DIFaddend and time-consuming to evaluate, thereby guiding the acquisition.
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A proposed advantage of the }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{offer major advantages }\DIFdelend in this role \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{primarily through strong }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{is relatively better }\DIFaddend low-data performance \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{compared to classical }\gls{ml} \DIFadd{optimization methods}\DIFaddend .
Their \gls{icl} capability enables task demonstration with minimal prompt examples while leveraging chemical knowledge from pre-training to generate accurate predictions. 
This allows \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{llm} \DIFaddend to compensate for sparse experimental data effectively. 
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{However, }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{are less robust than }\gls{gpr} \DIFadd{due to their tendency to hallucinate.
}\DIFaddend 

\textcite{ramos2023bayesian} demonstrated the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{viability of this paradigm through a simple yet effective }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{use of this low-data regime through a }\DIFaddend framework that combines \gls{icl} using only one example in the prompt with a \gls{bo} workflow. 
Their \gls{bo}-\gls{icl} approach uses \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{few-shot }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$k$-shot }\DIFaddend examples formatted as question-answer pairs, where the \gls{llm} generates candidate solutions conditioned on prior successful iterations. 
These candidates are ranked using an acquisition function, with top-$k$ selections integrated into subsequent prompts to refine predictions iteratively. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Remarkably, this method achieved high performance in optimizing catalytic reaction conditions, even matching the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Interestingly, in the reported experiments, the method performed competitively, matching }\DIFaddend top-1 accuracies \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{observed in experimental benchmarks }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{on the evaluated benchmarks compared to classical }\gls{bo} \DIFadd{methods}\DIFaddend . This emphasizes the potential of \glspl{llm} as accessible, \gls{icl} optimizers when coupled with well-designed prompts.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{To }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Trying to }\DIFaddend address limitations in base \glspl{llm}’ inherent chemical knowledge---particularly their grasp of specialized representations like \gls{smiles} or structure-property mappings---\textcite{yu2025collaborative} introduced a hybrid architecture augmenting pre-trained \glspl{llm} with task-specific embedding and prediction layers. 
These layers, fine-tuned on domain data, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{align }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{aligned }\DIFaddend latent representations of input-output pairs (denoted as \texttt{<x>} and \texttt{<y>} in prompts), enabling the model to map chemical structures and properties into a unified, interpretable space. Crucially, the added layers \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{enhance }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{were reported to improve }\DIFaddend chemical reasoning without sacrificing the flexibility of \gls{icl}, allowing the system to adapt to trends across iterations, similarly to what was done by \textcite{ramos2023bayesian}. 
In their evaluations of molecular optimization benchmarks, such as the \gls{pmo} \autocite{gao2022sample}, they revealed \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{improvements }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{that }\gls{llm}\DIFadd{-based methods matched or improved }\DIFaddend over conventional methods, including \gls{bo}-\gls{gp}, \gls{rl} methods, and \gls{ga}.


\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{yu2025collaborative} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
further highlighted the framework’s extensibility to diverse black-box optimization challenges beyond chemistry. 
This represents one of the most important advantages of using }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{as orchestrators of the optimization process. 
The flexibility of natural language in this process enables the procedure to be applied to any optimization process. 
In contrast, classical methods are constrained to the specific task for which they are designed due to the need to train the surrogate model.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{LLMs as Next Candidate Generators}

Recent studies \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{demonstrate the potential of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{explore the possibility of using }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{to enhance }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in enhancing }\DIFaddend \glspl{ea} \autocite{lu2024generative} and \gls{bo} \autocite{amin2025towards} frameworks by leveraging their embedded chemical knowledge and ability to integrate prior information, thereby \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{potentially }\DIFaddend reducing computational effort while improving output quality \DIFaddbegin \autocite{lu2024generative}\DIFaddend .
Within \glspl{ea}, \glspl{llm} refine molecular candidates through mutations (modifying molecular substructures) or crossovers (combining parent molecules). 
In \gls{bo} frameworks, they serve as acquisition \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{functions}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{policies}\DIFaddend , utilizing surrogate model predictions---both mean and uncertainty---to select optimal molecules or reaction conditions for evaluation.

For molecule optimization, \textcite{yu2025collaborative} introduced \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\modelname{MultiModel}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\modelname{MultiMol}}\DIFaddend , a dual-\gls{llm} system where one model proposes candidates and the other supplies domain knowledge (see \Cref{sec:opt-llm-know-source}). 
By fine-tuning the \enquote{worker} \gls{llm} to recognize molecular scaffolds and target properties, and expanding the training pool to include a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{million-size }\DIFdelend pre-training dataset \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, they achieved hit rates }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{of $\sim1$ million samples, they report hit rates (percentage of generated molecules that meet the target properties under a certain threshold) }\DIFaddend exceeding $90\%$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. 
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{on their evaluation set.
}

\DIFaddend Similarly, \textcite{wang2024efficient} developed \modelname{MoLLEO}, integrating an \gls{llm} into an \gls{ea} to replace random mutations with \gls{llm}-guided modifications. Here, \modelname{GPT-4} generated optimized offspring from parent molecules \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, significantly accelerating convergence }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{that converged faster }\DIFaddend to high fitness scores \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in the reported experiments}\DIFaddend . 
Notably, while domain-specialized models (\modelname{BioT5}, \modelname{MoleculeSTM}) underperformed, the general-purpose \modelname{GPT-4} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{excelled---a finding that underscores the context-dependent }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{excelled---suggesting the }\DIFaddend utility of \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{In a related approach, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{lu2024generative} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
showed that well-designed prompts---incorporating task-specific constraints, objectives, and few-shot examples---enable general }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{(\modelname{Claude\--3.5-Sonnet}, \modelname{o1-preview}) to generate high-quality candidates without fine-tuning, outperforming both random selection and vanilla }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{ga} %%%
\DIFdel{in functional }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{tmc} %%%
\DIFdel{design}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{may be context-dependent}\DIFaddend .


\subsubsection{LLMs as Prior Knowledge Sources} \label{sec:opt-llm-know-source}

A key advantage of integrating \glspl{llm} into optimization frameworks is their ability to encode and deploy prior knowledge within the optimization loop. 
As illustrated in \Cref{fig:optimization}, this knowledge can be directed into either the surrogate model or candidate generation module, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{significantly }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{potentially }\DIFaddend reducing the number of optimization steps required through high-quality guidance \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{if the feedback is useful}\DIFaddend .

For example, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{yu2025collaborative} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
deployed a }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{research} %%%
\DIFdel{agent that leverages \modelname{Google} search and \modelname{RDKit} to verify and rank molecules generated by }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{worker} %%%
\DIFdel{agents against target features and properties. 
Their results demonstrate substantial improvements when this filtering mechanism is applied.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Similarly, }\DIFdelend \textcite{cisse2025language0based} introduced \modelname{BORA}, which contextualizes conventional black-box \gls{bo} using an \gls{llm}. \modelname{BORA} maintains standard \gls{bo} as the core driver\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend but strategically activates the \gls{llm} when progress stalls. 
This leverages the model’s \gls{icl} capabilities to hypothesize promising search regions and propose new samples, regulated by a lightweight heuristic policy that manages costs and incorporates domain knowledge (or user input). 
Evaluations on synthetic benchmarks\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{such as the catalyst optimization task }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, such as optimizing the catalyst mixture }\DIFaddend for hydrogen generation\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend show that \modelname{BORA} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{accelerates exploration, improves convergence, and outperforms existing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{was reported to accelerate exploration, and outperforms the }\DIFaddend \gls{llm}-\gls{bo} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{hybrids}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{hybrid methods evaluated}\DIFaddend .

To \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{potentially }\DIFaddend enhance the task-specific knowledge of the \gls{llm} generating feedback, \textcite{zhang2025large} fine-tuned a \modelname{Llama-2-7B} model using a multitask \gls{qa} dataset. This dataset was created with instructions from \modelname{GPT-4}. 
The resulting model served as a human assistant or operated within an active learning loop, thereby accelerating the exploration of new reaction spaces (see \Cref{sec:retrosynthesis}). 
However, as the authors note, even this task-specialized \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{llm} \DIFaddend produces suboptimal suggestions for optimization tasks. 
They remain prone to hallucination and cannot assist with unreported reactions, but still \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, they improve for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{improved upon pure classical methods in }\DIFaddend most of the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{applications, using pure classical methods}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{evaluated applications}\DIFaddend .


\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{How to Face }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Approaching }\DIFaddend Optimization Problems\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{?}\DIFdelend }

Published works explore different ways of using \glspl{llm} for optimization problems in chemistry, from simple approaches, such as just prompting the model with some initial random set of experimental candidates and iterating \autocite{ramos2023bayesian}, to fine-tuning models in \gls{bo} fashion \autocite{rankovic2025gollum0}. 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The most efficient initial point is by relying entirely on a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A pragmatic initial step is to try a purely }\DIFaddend \gls{icl} approach, which allows one to obtain a first signal rapidly. 
Such \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{initial results will enable to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{results help }\DIFaddend determine whether a more complex, computationally intensive approach is necessary or whether prompt engineering is reliable \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{enough }\DIFdelend for the application. 
Fine-tuning can be used as a way to enhance the chemical knowledge of the \glspl{llm} and can lead to improvements in optimization tasks where the model requires such knowledge to choose or generate better candidates. 
Fine-tuning might not be a game-changer for other approaches that rely more on sampling methods \autocite{wang2025llm0augmented}.

While some initial works showed that \glspl{llm} trained specifically on chemistry perform better for optimization tasks \autocite{kristiadi2024sober}, other works showed that a \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{gpm} \DIFaddend such as \modelname{GPT-4} combined with an \gls{ea} outperformed all other models \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \autocite{wang2024efficient}%%%
\DIFdel{.
Is it better to incorporate a general model or a chemistry }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{lm} %%%
\DIFdel{into the optimization frameworks?
We hypothesize that for models of the same size (in number of parameters) and similar training size---attending to }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{pflop}%%%
\DIFdel{---a chemical }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{lm} %%%
\DIFdel{(a specialized model) will consistently outperform general models. 
If the models differ significantly in size, the larger model will typically perform better.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \autocite{wang2024efficient, macknight2025pre0trained}\DIFadd{.
}


\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Limitations}}

\DIFadd{Current }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{for chemical optimization, to date, exhibit a pronounced volatility, rarely yielding stable performance gains. 
Their outcomes are highly sensitive to prompt phrasing, and a lack of calibrated uncertainty estimates prevents their use for principled acquisition strategies. }\autocite{liu2024large}
\DIFadd{Furthermore, these models frequently produce hallucinations and violate critical constraints, leading to invalid chemical structures or the proposal of unsafe and infeasible experimental conditions. 
Alleged improvements often hinge on narrow benchmarks, extensive pre- and post-processing, or access to downstream oracles, rather than on robust chemical reasoning. 
Within hybrid pipelines, the specific contribution of the }\gls{llm} \DIFadd{becomes difficult to isolate.
}

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Correct Prompt Sensitivity}}\DIFadd{: A key challenge is achieving prompt invariance, where the ranking of candidates remains stable under controlled rephrasings. Promising research directions include developing rigorous invariance tests and moving beyond discrete tokens to leverage models' more stable hidden states }\autocite{rankovic2025gollum0}\DIFadd{.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{More robust evaluations}}\DIFadd{: Future work should develop more robust frameworks that assess oracle realism, conduct thorough leakage audits to detect data contamination, and integrate uncertainty-aware metrics to better quantify risk and reliability.
    }\item \textbf{\DIFadd{Ablations for the specific LLM role}}\DIFadd{: There is a critical need for systematic ablation studies that isolate core }\gls{llm} \DIFadd{performance from enhancements like }\gls{rag}\DIFadd{, external scorers, or tool use. Such studies are essential for fairly comparing general and fine-tuned }\glspl{llm} \DIFadd{and for understanding the source of performance gains.
}\end{itemize}  \DIFaddend \section{Implications of GPMs: Education, Safety, and Ethics}
\label{sec:implications}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The advent of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{As }\DIFaddend \glspl{gpm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in the chemical sciences marks a paradigm shift that extends beyond methodological advances to fundamentally alter the conceptual frameworks through which scientific knowledge is produced and validated.
As these models permeate }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{integrate into chemistry }\DIFaddend education and research, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{their transformative potential is closely linked to critical challengesin cultivating discerning learners, mitigating emergent risksin automated discovery, and navigating ethical dilemmas arising from biased systems.
Here, these tripartite implications are examined, and it is argued that responsible integration of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{requires not only technical innovation but also rigorous pedagogical and regulatory frameworks.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{they bring transformative potential alongside critical challenges. Responsible deployment requires addressing pedagogical practices, chemical-specific safety risks, and ethical considerations unique to scientific knowledge systems.
}\DIFaddend
