\DIFdel{pipeline to deliver curriculum-aligned answers. 
The I-Digest team }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{Jablonka2023} %%%
\DIFdel{introduced a platform that generates lecture summaries and follow-up questions to support continuous learning. 
Some systems also integrate specialized components to improve personalization and continuity in the learning process. 
Although not designed for chemistry specifically, general-purpose platforms such as TutorLLM }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{li2025tutorllm0} %%%
\DIFdel{(generating personalized content based on the learning progress) and LearnMate }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{wang2025learnmate0} %%%
\DIFdel{(creating learning plans and giving feedback) demonstrate how large models can be embedded in structured educational frameworks. 
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Anthropic's Claude for Education }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{AnthropicEducation} %%%
\DIFdel{offers a purpose-built platform for higher education with a }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{Learning Mode} %%%
\DIFdel{that uses the Socratic method to guide students rather than directly giving the answer to their queries. The intention here is to promote active learning and mitigate the risks of passive tool use. 
While such systems illustrate the potential of structured }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{-based learning environments, fully integrated applications remain rare---particularly in domain-specific contexts such as chemistry. 
Furthermore, current models must still strengthen their robustness and domain-specific reasoning before they can be trusted in demanding fields, including chemistry.}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Outlook and }\DIFdelend Limitations}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{While }\DIFdelend \glspl{gpm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{offer promising opportunities for chemistry education, their use also raises critical ethical and pedagogical concerns.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{A first concern is the lack of transparency in how these models generate responses. 
Although }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{produce fluent and plausible explanations, they do so without genuine understanding---and often with no clear indication of uncertainty or possible errors. 
This can lead students to accept incorrect or misleading information as fact, particularly when the output appears confident or authoritative. Over time, such interactions may normalize uncritical acceptance and discourage studentsfrom questioning, verifying, or reflecting on what they are told.In scientific education, where reasoning, skepticism, and an evidence-based mindset are essential, this poses a serious threat to the development of informed and independent   learners.}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{lack transparency in sources and confidence, enabling hallucinations to mislead students.}\DIFaddend \autocite{marcus2025will, kosmyna2025your} \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{A related but distinct issue is the risk of over-reliance and deskilling. When students delegate a large portion of the learning process to generative tools, they might be able to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Over-reliance risks deskilling: students }\DIFaddend complete assignments without \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{engaging in the mental work needed to develop subject-specific competence. 
In such cases, }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{can disrupt the connection between concrete tasks---such as solving a problem or writing an explanation---and the broader educational goals they are meant to support, such as }\DIFdelend developing chemical understanding or analytical thinking\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{skills}\DIFdelend .\autocite{dung2025learning, Sharma2025role} \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Models are unreliable for grading chemistry work, particularly free-text reasoning and molecular diagrams.}\autocite{baral2025drawedumath0, Kortemeyer2024}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Today---and even more so in the future---students and learners will increasingly use }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{, both in education and in their future professions.Attempting to restrict their use is neither realistic nor educationally meaningful.
    Therefore, educators must guide their use thoughtfully and adapt both the learning process and assessment practices accordingly.
    Furthermore, the question of what to learn needs to be redefined. 
What we learn must increasingly center around the development of critical thinking, creativity, and logical reasoning---skills that remain essential and irreplaceable, even---or perhaps especially---in the age of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Open Challenges}}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Responsible Integration Strategies.}} \DIFadd{Developing pedagogical frameworks for thoughtful }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{integration in chemistry curricula, adapting assessment to emphasize critical evaluation over rote completion.
    }

    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Chemistry-Specific Systems.}} \DIFadd{Building platforms that integrate chemical structure recognition, spectroscopic data interpretation, and laboratory safety protocols into tutoring and assessment workflows.
    }

    \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Critical Competence Training.}} \DIFadd{Teaching students to evaluate chemical plausibility, identify hallucinated reactions, and creatively extend model outputs}\DIFaddend .\autocite{klein2025rethink}

    \DIFaddbegin \item \textbf{\DIFadd{Validated Assessment Tools.}} \DIFadd{Developing robust grading systems for chemistry-specific tasks: mechanism proposals, spectral interpretation, synthesis planning.
}\end{itemize}


\DIFaddend \subsection{Safety} \label{sec:safety}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{figures/safety_chemrev_fig.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{A conceptual schematic depicting \gls{ai} risk factors in chemical science}. As one traverses through the \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{game-like }\DIFdelendFL scientific process, \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{illustrated as a game, }\DIFaddendFL there are various obstacles to encountering \gls{ai} exacerbated risks. The path to superaligned chemical \gls{ai}-assistants is obfuscated by unexplored chemical space.}
    \label{fig:safety-overview}
\end{figure}

\noindent \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A growing coalition within the scientific community has sounded a call to action: }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{poses existential risks that deserve the same urgent attention as pandemics and nuclear war }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{cais2023statement}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
This call, formalized in a statement signed by hundreds of prominent }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{researchers, reflects mounting recognition that advanced }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{systems could fundamentally alter, or threaten, human civilization. 
While the ongoing discourse has focused on abstract notions of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{agi}%%%
\DIFdel{, the immediate risks may emerge through the integration of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{into specific domains where the stakes are already high }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{morris2023levels}%%%
\DIFdel{.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Chemistry represents one such domain. 
The rapid integration of }\DIFdelend \glspl{gpm} in chemistry \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is a dual-edged sword. 
Although these technologies can accelerate discovery , they also introduce unprecedented }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{are double-edged: they accelerate discovery while potentially amplifying chemical safety }\DIFaddend risks. From democratizing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{access to hazardous chemical }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{hazardous synthesis }\DIFaddend knowledge to enabling autonomous \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{synthesis of dangerous compounds, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{systems could lower barriers for misuse , whether intentional or accidental. 
}%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{alone may not create new risks }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{peppin2024reality}%%%
\DIFdel{, but they can amplify existing ones (see }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{dangerous compound production, these systems lower barriers to misuse (}\DIFaddend \Cref{fig:safety-overview}). \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Even in this amplified context, the ability of these systems to pose meaningful safety risks in practice is constrained by real-world limitations, including access to specialized lab }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Real-world constraints---specialized }\DIFaddend equipment, regulated \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{or scarce reagents, and, most critically, the }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{tacit knowledge}\autocite{Polanyi_2009} %%%
\DIFdel{required to execute complex chemical processes. 
Tacit knowledge, the expertise gained through hands-on experience and intuition, cannot be fully acquired from textbooks or datasets alone, as it is normally shared verbally and encompasses small learnings, often considered insignificant. 
This gap between theoretical }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{outputs and practical execution underscores why risks, though serious, might remain manageable with proactive safeguards.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Ultimately, mitigating these threats requires a nuanced balance of fostering innovation while embedding safety at the architectural, operational, and governance levels}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reagents, tacit expertise---currently limit risks, but }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{are progressively overcoming these barriers}\DIFaddend .

\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Evaluating }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Chemical-Specific }\DIFaddend Risk Amplification\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in the Chemical Discovery Cycle}\DIFdelend }

\paragraph{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Dual Use}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Information Access and Synthesis Planning}\DIFaddend } 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A critical question is whether }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{provide maliciously acting novices with new avenues to obtain harmful knowledge beyond what is already easily accessible (e.g., via the internet)}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{sandbrink2023artificial}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
Preliminary research has explored whether }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{can exacerbate biorisks, a concern that extends analogously to chemical safety under the }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{information access} %%%
\DIFdel{threat model }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{peppin2024reality}%%%
\DIFdel{. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{urbina2022dual} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
explored how their de novo molecular generator, \modelname{MegaSyn} , could be used to design toxic chemical agents by adjusting the reward system of the model to prefer compounds with greater toxicityand bioactivity. While their model predicted VX (toxic nerve agent) and other chemical warfare agents, the actual synthesis of such compounds requires expertise, controlled precursors, and specialized equipment that is far beyond the capabilities of most non-state actors.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Recent evaluations by \modelname{OpenAI} and \modelname{Anthropic} have systematically assessed how their models can facilitate the creation of biological threats. 
In an evaluation of their Deep Research System (a multi-agent architecture), \modelname{OpenAI} classified the system to be medium-risk for chemical and biological threat-creation}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{can lower cognitive barriers to accessing dangerous chemical knowledge. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{urbina2022dual} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
demonstrated that molecular generators like \modelname{MegaSyn} could design toxic agents including VX when reward functions prioritize toxicity}\DIFaddend . \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \autocite{openai2024building} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{As discussed previously, a key barrier that prevents such models from exceeding the assessed risk threshold is the acquisition of }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{tacit knowledge}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
However, this system demonstrated modest improvements in troubleshooting and the acquisition of tacit knowledge. 
Although it still fell short of expert-level performance, these findings suggest that models are making progress toward overcoming this critical hurdle.
They also evaluated \modelname{GPT-4} 's impact on experts and students across five }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Recent evaluations of \modelname{GPT-4} and \modelname{Claude 4 Opus} for }\DIFaddend biological threat creation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{stages: ideation, acquisition, magnification, formulation, and release. }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{openai2024building} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Their key finding was that biorisk information is widely accessiblewithout }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{and that practical constraints such as wet lab access or domain expertise are more limiting than information scarcity. 
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{\modelname{Anthropic}'s parallel assessment of \modelname{Claude 4 Opus} focused specifically on biological risks through red-teaming with bio-defense experts, multi-step agentic evaluations, and explicit testing of bioinformatics tool integration }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{anthropic2025system}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
Their findings align with \modelname{OpenAI}'s assessment of \modelname{GPT-4}, and conclude that current systems remain constrained by physical barriers. Both studies emphasize that supply chain control of chemicals, the flow of goods (e.g., chemical reagents) from suppliers to consumers, remains crucial as these systems continue to evolve and barriers to accessing knowledge are continuously lowered.For example, although \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{he2023control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
showed that }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{found that while information is already accessible, }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{improve troubleshooting and help acquire tacit knowledge previously limiting non-experts.}\autocite{openai2024building, anthropic2025system} \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{he2023control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
showed }\DIFaddend \glspl{llm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{can }\DIFdelend generate pathways for explosives \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{like }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(}\DIFaddend \gls{petn}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{or nerve agents like sarin, the supply chain of obtaining precursor chemicals for weapons like sarin is tightly regulated. 
In addition, access to lab infrastructure like fume hoods or inert environments is not trivial to obtain for non-experts.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{In the status quo it remains true that these risks are mitigated by material and logistical hurdles }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{sandbrink2023artificial}%%%
\DIFdel{. Nonetheless, existing information access or presumed barriers to accessing materials are not an argument for }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{complacency. 
Models that lower the technical or cognitive barriers to weaponization even incrementally risk acting }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{) and nerve agents (sarin). While precursor supply chains remain regulated, }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{that incrementally lower technical barriers act }\DIFaddend as force multipliers for malicious actors. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Moreover, a red-teaming (discussed in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{para:red_teaming}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) effort proved that these practical constraints are circumventable and show that real world checks are prone to failure}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Chemistry-specific agents like \modelname{ChemCrow}}\autocite{bran2024augmenting} \DIFadd{and \modelname{Coscientist}}\autocite{boiko2023autonomous} \DIFadd{demonstrate how }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{can plan and execute complex syntheses---capabilities extending to dangerous compounds}\DIFaddend .

\paragraph{Hallucinations \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in Chemical Contexts}\DIFaddend } 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Another critical risk of }\DIFdelend \glspl{gpm} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is their propensity for hallucination, leading to factually incorrect outputs}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{can hallucinate non-existent reactions, falsify safety protocols, and generate plausible-sounding but incorrect procedures}\DIFaddend .\autocite{pantha2024challenges, ji2023survey} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{These errors risk propagating misinformation, such as inventing non-existent chemical reactions or falsifying safety protocols. Additionally, }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{suffer from temporal misalignment ; their }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In chemistry, these errors risk laboratory accidents or synthesis failures that waste resources. Temporal misalignment due to }\DIFaddend static training data \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{renders outputs obsolete in fast-evolving fields like drug discovery }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{pantha2024challenges}%%%
\DIFdel{. Therefore, their accuracy in chemistry decays sharply for research published after the training cutoff, underscoring the need for real-time verification systems}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{means chemical knowledge becomes outdated as new reactions, hazards, and regulations emerge}\DIFaddend .

\paragraph{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Indirect Cyberattack Risk}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Autonomous Laboratory Risks}\DIFaddend } 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The convergence of individual steps of the chemical discovery cycle in autonomous laboratory systems or cloud-based laboratories represents a high-risk scenario }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{rouleau2025risks}%%%
\DIFdel{.
Beyond traditional cybersecurity threats, these systems face a critical timeline mismatch : }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Autonomous laboratories controlled by }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{face cybersecurity vulnerabilities. Compromised systems could be manipulated to synthesize hazardous compounds. The timeline mismatch between }\DIFaddend \gls{ai} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{systems are projected to achieve superhuman hacking capabilities by 2027 while operating within inadequately secured infrastructure }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{dean2025security}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
For autonomous laboratories, this means }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{systemscapable of designing hazardous compounds could be compromised by external actors.}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{capabilities and infrastructure security creates risk windows where adversaries exploit inadequately protected systems.}\autocite{rouleau2025risks, dean2025security}
\DIFaddend 


\subsubsection{Existing Approaches to Safety} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Adversarial testing and red teaming have become prominent methods for evaluating the safety of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{systems, even in the chemical domain (see \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:evals}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
). 
While such evaluations are valuable to identify weaknesses in }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{, they are inherently reactive. 
These approaches highlight failures only after a model is trained or deployed, rather than embedding safety into the model's architecture. 
Moreover, adversarial testing is often unsystematic and relies on human-curated test cases that may not range across all potential risks, particularly in complex domains such as chemistry. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{To move beyond reactive measures, an emerging field of safety research explores machine }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{unlearning}%%%
\DIFdel{, a technique that selectively removes hazardous knowledge from a model's training data }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{barez2025open}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
However, this approach faces significant }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Red-teaming evaluations identify vulnerabilities but are reactive, not preventive. \modelname{ChemCrow}'s safeguards block known controlled substances, yet \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{he2023control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
demonstrated these protections rely on post-query web searches rather than embedded constraints---easily circumventable. Machine unlearning attempts to remove hazardous knowledge face fundamental }\DIFaddend challenges in chemistry\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{. First, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{: }\DIFaddend defining \enquote{dangerous chemical knowledge} is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{non-trivial because chemical properties are }\DIFdelend context-dependent \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
Seemingly benign compounds like bleach can become }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{(bleach is benign alone, }\DIFaddend hazardous when combined\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{or misused. 
Second, unlearning risks can lead to a model 's utility degradation, and the resulting challenge of balancing the trade-off between safety and functionality remains unresolved. 
This balance is especially challenging for }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm}%%%
\DIFdel{, which must achieve broad applicability with strict safety constraints. }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{A more implicit safety strategy is alignment, which aims to steer model behavior toward human values through techniques like }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{), and removal risks degrading model utility for legitimate research. Alignment through }\DIFaddend \gls{rlhf} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{or Constitutional }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} \autocite{bai2022constitutional}%%%
\DIFdel{. Although alignment can reduce harmful outputs , it may not generalize well to novel or domain-specific threats. 
For instance, a chemically aligned model might refuse to synthesize a known toxin, but could still be manipulated into suggesting precursor chemicals.  
Moreover, even after undergoing alignment training, they are still prone to produce risky and harmful content and will always be susceptible to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reduces harmful outputs but remains vulnerable to }\DIFaddend jailbreaks\autocite{kuntz2025os-harm, yona2024stealing, lynch2025agentic} \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and fails to generalize to novel threats (models might refuse known toxins while suggesting precursors)}\DIFaddend .


\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{In an effort to train trustworthy models, many }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{researchers have turned to }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{interpretability}%%%
\DIFdel{, an approach that aims to explain how the computations }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{are linked to the output. }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{cunningham2023sparse} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{However, in a recent global evaluation of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{safety, \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{bengio2025international} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
argue that }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{sota} %%%
\DIFdel{interpretability tools have not proven their reliability in understanding models to modify them to alleviate safety risks.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{makelov2023subspace}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{Challenges in Developing Safeguards}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Even chemistry-specific }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{llm} %%%
\DIFdel{agents like \modelname{ChemCrow} }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{bran2024augmenting} %%%
\DIFdel{or \modelname{Coscientist}}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{boiko2023autonomous} %%%
\DIFdel{exhibit vulnerabilities. For instance, \modelname{ChemCrow}'s safeguards block known controlled substances, yet \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{he2023control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
demonstrated a flaw in its safety protocols. 
The agent's refusals are reactive rather than proactive, as they rely on post-query web search checks rather than embedded safeguards. 
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The capabilities and ensuing risks posed by }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{need to be contextualized around user intent }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{tang2024prioritizing}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
Under a paradigm of malicious intent, the user intentionally creates a dangerous situation or application using the tool. 
However, an uninformed, benign user (e.g., a chemistry undergraduate student)could be unaware of the dangers posed by a given model output and unable to differentiate hallucinated responses. 
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{Solutions}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The gaps in current }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{safety measures reveal a pressing need for proactive frameworks that address technical and systemic risks. }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{bengio2025international}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{Regulatory Framework for Chemical }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{Models}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Drawing from emerging biosecurity governance models, as a first step, chemical }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Chemical }\gls{gpm} \DIFaddend oversight should focus on \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a narrow class of }\DIFdelend \enquote{advanced chemical models} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{that meet }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{meeting }\DIFaddend specific risk thresholds\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.  
Similar to proposed biological model regulations, these could include }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{: }\DIFaddend models trained on \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{not widely accessible, particularly sensitive chemical data}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{bloomfield2024ai}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{sensitive synthesis data, systems demonstrating autonomous synthesis planning capabilities, or agents with laboratory control interfaces}\DIFaddend . This targeted approach \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is preferable to regulating all chemical }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{models because it avoids creating compliance burdens that would disproportionately affect }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{avoids burdening }\DIFaddend low-risk research while capturing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the systems that actually pose security concerns.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{concerning systems---analogous to proposed biological model regulations.}\autocite{bloomfield2024ai}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Existing Institutional Efforts}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Recent governmental initiatives have begun to address }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{safety concerns.The US }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{Safety Institute }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{nist2024safety} %%%
\DIFdel{and the UK }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{Safety Institute have been tasked with designing safety evaluations for frontier models and researching catastrophic risks from }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{systems . 
In contrast, the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{eu} %%%
\DIFdel{has taken a more pragmatic regulatory approach through the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{eu} \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{Act }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{EU2024regulation}%%%
\DIFdel{, which classifies }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{systems by risk levels and imposes obligations ranging from transparency requirements to prohibited uses. These nascent efforts, while promising, face significant limitations. National institutes operate within frameworks that may prioritize domestic interests over global safety. While more comprehensive in scope, the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{eu} \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{Act focuses primarily on general }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{applications rather than domain-specific risks such as chemical synthesis, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Developing chemistry-aware guardrails requires systems that evaluate chemical plausibility, safety, }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{its risk classification system may not adequately capture the unique dual-use nature of chemical }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{models}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{regulatory compliance before providing synthesis information. These technical safeguards should integrate real-time hazard assessment checking outputs against controlled substance databases, context-aware refusal mechanisms understanding legitimate versus dangerous use cases, precursor tracking identifying suspicious synthesis pathway queries, and audit logging enabling forensic analysis of concerning interactions. Establishing review processes for }\gls{gpm}\DIFadd{-assisted chemical research analogous to institutional biosafety committees provides essential institutional oversight. Researchers deploying chemical }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{with autonomous capabilities should undergo safety review, particularly for autonomous synthesis planning and execution, large-scale screening for bioactive compounds, or novel chemical class exploration without expert oversight}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Future Institutional Oversight and Transparency}} %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{A critical step forward is establishing neutral and independent regulatory bodies to oversee }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{development}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{International coordination remains critical for effective chemical }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{governance}\DIFaddend . Unlike self-regulation, which risks conflicts of interest \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, an }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and competitive pressure toward laxity, an international oversight body---analogous to organizations governing nuclear materials or biological weapons---could harmonize safety standards across jurisdictions. Such an }\DIFaddend \gls{iaio} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{comprised of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{comprising }\DIFaddend \gls{ai} researchers, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{policymakers, ethicists, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{chemists, policymakers, }\DIFaddend and security experts could \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{harmonize standards and prevent a }%DIFDELCMD < \enquote{race to the bottom} %%%
\DIFdel{in regulatory laxity }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{trager2023international}%%%
\DIFdel{. Such a body could mandate }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{establish }\DIFaddend pre-approval \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{requirements }\DIFaddend for high-risk \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{research (like }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{agi}%%%
\DIFdel{-aligned projects)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{chemical }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{development}\DIFaddend , similar to institutional review boards \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{that exist in the biomedical researchspace }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{pistono2016unethical}%%%
\DIFdel{.Precedents for this }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{in biomedical research.}\autocite{trager2023international} \DIFadd{Precedents }\DIFaddend exist in \gls{cern}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, which tries to balance civilian duties }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{'s approach to balancing civilian research }\DIFaddend with dual-use \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{risks }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{risk management }\DIFaddend and nuclear non-proliferation treaties \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{that tie }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{tying }\DIFaddend market access to compliance.
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \autocite{cern_nuclear_safeguards_2024} 
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Effective governance also requires binding enforcement mechanisms. 
One approach is conditional market access, where }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{products and precursors can only be traded internationally if certified by the }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{iaio}%%%
\DIFdel{. 
Participating states would then enact domestic laws to align with these standards, ensuring corporate and national compliance. 
This model leverages economic incentives rather than voluntary guidelines to enforce safety. Transparency must also be enforced, particularly in red-teaming and safety testing. 
Currently, many companies conduct red-teaming privately, with no obligation to disclose the findings or corrective actions.
To address this, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{developers should be required to }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{Transparency requirements should mandate that chemical }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{developers }\DIFaddend publicly report red-teaming results \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and undergo third-party safety audits for high-stakes applications.}%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{carlini2025career}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The risks posed by }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{in chemistry demand immediate action from governments and institutions. 
Yet these challenges also invite a deeper question: }\textit{\DIFdel{Is the integration of }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{into scientific discovery fundamentally advancing our understanding or merely accelerating the production of epistemic noise?}} 
%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{As \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\textcite{narayanan2025why} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
cautioned, }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai}%%%
\DIFdel{’s predictive abilities often obscure its inability to explain underlying mechanisms. 
In chemistry, this tension is acute: }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai}%%%
\DIFdel{-driven tools may }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{for dangerous synthesis queries, safety evaluation methodologies and thresholds, known vulnerabilities and mitigation strategies, and training data sources documenting chemical knowledge scope. The fundamental tension remains: }\glspl{gpm} \DIFaddend optimize reactions or design toxins with equal \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ease}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{facility}\DIFaddend , but their black-box nature complicates accountability\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and obscures causal relationships. True progress may require safeguards against misuse and a reevaluation of whether }%DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai}%%%
\DIFdel{’s role in science should be expansive or deliberately constrained}\DIFdelend . \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Progress requires not just safeguards but deliberate constraints on chemical }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{capabilities in high-risk domains.
}\DIFaddend 


\subsection{Ethics} \label{sec:ethics}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The deployment of }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{in the chemical sciences raises several critical ethical concerns that require careful consideration. 
These issues range from perpetuating harmful biases to environmental impacts and intellectual property concerns }%DIFDELCMD < \autocite{crawford2021atlas}%%%
\DIFdel{.}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{gpm} \DIFadd{deployment in chemistry raises ethical concerns requiring careful consideration: from bias in chemical knowledge to environmental costs of computation.}\autocite{crawford2021atlas}
\DIFaddend 


\subsubsection{Environmental Impact \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and Climate Ethics}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{of GPMs}\DIFaddend }

The computational requirements for training and deploying \glspl{gpm} contribute to environmental degradation through excessive energy consumption and carbon emissions. \autocite{spotte-smith2025considering, nature2023carbon} 
These computational resources are often powered by fossil fuel-based energy sources, which directly contribute to anthropogenic climate change. \autocite{strubell2019energy} 
The emphasis on \gls{ai} research has superseded some \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{of the }\DIFdelend commitments made by big technological companies to carbon neutrality. 
For example, Google rescinded its commitment to carbon neutrality amid a surge in \gls{ai} usage (65$\%$ increase in carbon emissions between \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{2021-24}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{2021--24}\DIFaddend ) and funding.\autocite{bhuiyan2025google} 
Additionally, the water consumption for cooling data centers that support these models is another concern, particularly in regions facing water scarcity. \autocite{mytton2021data}

The irony is particularly stark when considering that in the chemical sciences, these models are used to address climate-related challenges, such as the development of sustainable materials or carbon capture technologies. 
As a scientific community, we must grapple with the questions about the sustainability of current \gls{ai} development trajectories and consider more efficient and renewable approaches to model development and deployment. \autocite{kolbert2024obscene}


\subsubsection{Copyright Infringement and Plagiarism Concerns}

\glspl{gpm} are typically trained on a vast corpora of copyrighted scientific literature, patents, and proprietary databases, often without explicit permission, a practice that has sparked legal disputes, such as \textit{Getty Images v.\ Stability \gls{ai}}, where plaintiffs allege unauthorized scraping of protected content. \autocite{kirchhubel2024intellectual} Developers at \modelname{OpenAI} claimed in a statement to the \gls{uk} House of Lords that training \gls{sota} models is \enquote{impossible} without copyrighted material, highlighting a fundamental tension between \gls{ip} law and \gls{ai} advancement. \autocite{openai2023written} 
In the chemical sciences, this challenge persists through the training of models on experimental results from pay-walled journals. 
A potential resolution to this in the scientific sphere lies in the expansion of open-access research frameworks. 
Initiatives like the \gls{cas} Common Chemistry database provide legally clear training data while maintaining attribution.
\glspl{llm} have shown a high propensity to regurgitate elements from their training data. 
When generating text, models may reproduce near-verbatim fragments of training data without citation, effectively obscuring intellectual contributions.\autocite{bender2021dangers} 
While some praise \glspl{gpm} for overcoming \enquote{blank-page syndrome} for early-career scientists \autocite{altmae2023artificial}, others warn that uncritical reliance on their outputs risks eroding scientific rigor.\autocite{donker2023dangers}


\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Bias and Discrimination}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Biases}\DIFaddend }

\glspl{gpm} inherit and amplify harmful prejudices and stereotypes present in their training data, which pose significant risks when applied translationally to medicinal chemistry and biochemistry. \autocite{spotte-smith2025considering, yang2024demographic, omiye2023large} 
These models can perpetuate inaccurate and harmful assumptions based on race and gender about drug efficacy, toxicity, and disease susceptibility, leading to misdiagnosis and mistreatment. \autocite{chen2023algorithmic} 
Historical medical literature contains biased representations of how different populations respond to treatments, and \glspl{gpm} trained on such data can reinforce these misconceptions. \autocite{mittermaier2023bias}
The problem extends to broader contexts in chemical research. 
Biased models can influence research priorities, funding decisions, and the development of chemical tools in ways that systematically disadvantage the most vulnerable populations \autocite{dotan2019value0laden}. 

\paragraph{Solutions} The problem of bias can be best addressed through top-down reform. 
The data necessary to train unbiased models can only exist if clinical studies of drug efficacy are conducted on diverse populations in the real world.\autocite{criado-perez2019invisible} 
To complement improved data collection, standard evaluations for bias testing must be developed and mandated prior to deployment of \glspl{gpm}.


\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Democratization of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Access and }\DIFaddend Power \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Concentration}\DIFaddend } 

Although \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \gls{ai} %%%
\DIFdel{tools }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{gpm} \DIFaddend have the potential to democratize access to advanced chemical research capabilities, they may also concentrate power in the hands of a few large companies that control the frontier models. 
This concentration raises concerns about equitable access to research tools, particularly for researchers in smaller institutions with limited resources.\autocite{satariano2025a1i1}

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{As a community, we should ensure that the benefits of }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{in chemistry remain broadly accessible via public compute, open-weight models, and portable tooling. We should also insist on fair access terms and transparent benchmarks so that no single provider can gatekeep core research.
}



 \DIFaddend \section{Outlook and Conclusions}
\begin{figure}[htb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{figures/conclusion_figure_v2.pdf}
    \caption{\textbf{Evolution of \gls{gpm}-powered systems.} First \gls{gpm} applications directly used zero- or few-shot prompted or fine-tuned \glspl{gpm}. More complex tasks could be solved by combining multiple \glspl{gpm} in workflows where the execution trajectory is pre-determined. In agents, \glspl{gpm} autonomously decide on the execution trajectory and\DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, }\DIFaddendFL in this way\DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{, }\DIFaddendFL enable researchers to address open-ended tasks. Moving forward, coupling the validation closer to real-world objectives with further increased validation in better, custom user interfaces will enhance the impact of \glspl{gpm}. To ensure safe and ethical deployment, the community must engage with the broader public and policymakers to devise governance strategies.}
    \label{fig:conclusions}
\end{figure}

As we have explored in this review, \glspl{gpm}---especially \glspl{llm}---hold remarkable promise for the chemical sciences. 
The field \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{moved from using models in simplesingle calls to a }%DIFDELCMD < \glspl{gpm} %%%
\DIFdel{to developing workflows , in which a sequence of calls is performed, to increasingly complex autonomous agents in which the model decides on its own trajectory }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{has evolved from simple, single model calls to sophisticated workflows that chain multiple calls together, and further to the development of autonomous agents that determine their own problem-solving trajectories }\DIFaddend (see \Cref{fig:conclusions}).
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{To power those agents, increasingly powerful models are being built, including reasoning models that promise higher data efficiency. }\DIFdelend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Yet, several }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Applicability of GPMs}} \DIFadd{With all this flexibility, it becomes tempting to deploy }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{across a wide range of problems in the chemical sciences. However, as discussed in the previous sections, they are not always a direct replacement for existing approaches. In some cases, using a }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{may be unnecessary and inefficient. That said, there are clear scenarios where }\glspl{gpm} \DIFadd{offers distinct advantages. When data is unstructured or }\enquote{fuzzy}\DIFadd{---such as text-based descriptions or incomplete experimental records lacking explicit molecular structures---}\glspl{gpm}\DIFadd{, become valuable alternatives where specialized pipelines like }\glspl{gnn} \DIFadd{would be inapplicable. Similarly, in extremely low-data regimes where specialized pipelines or domain-specific foundation models would overfit, }\gls{gpm} \DIFadd{embeddings can provide more robust predictions than random baselines. Finally, for dynamic environments that require iterative reasoning, interpretation, and adjustment of the system states, }\gls{gpm}\DIFadd{-powered agents are well-suited. In contrast, when clean datasets are available and inductive biases are well-understood, domain-specific models typically offer superior performance and interpretability.
}


\paragraph{\DIFadd{Open Questions}} \DIFadd{Several }\DIFaddend fundamental questions remain unresolved. We do not understand if there are fundamental limits to what can be predicted, given the inherent unpredictability of chemical systems and the reliance on tacit knowledge. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{We }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{It remains unclear whether generative models achieve a genuine understanding of chemistry or merely excel at pattern recognition, a distinction obscured by the lack of methods to quantify chemical reasoning }\autocite{alampara2024probing}\DIFadd{. This uncertainty challenges the need for human-interpretable output, implying that the latent knowledge within hidden embeddings may be more significant }\autocite{hao2024training}\DIFadd{. Moreover, we }\DIFaddend do not know what new datasets and techniques need to be developed, given the fact that the knowledge we extract from already published data is approaching a limit.\autocite{silver2025welcome} New data most likely will be generated by agents learning from their own experience.
To optimize systems, we need to better understand the underlying structure of chemical data.
In many other fields, data distributions have been shaped by special driving forces. 
For example, evolution led to a direct link between sequence and fitness in biological sequences, which makes such datasets special. In chemistry\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, }\DIFaddend it is unclear what the \enquote{driving force} that shapes datasets is. 

It is also unclear how quickly these innovations will permeate the average chemistry lab, where the adoption of new technology depends on more than just predictive prowess. 
And we also do not know yet how we should interface with those models for the greatest effectiveness.
In addition, it is also unclear how far acceleration can take us, as nature imposes some natural speed limits: Some experiments simply take their time. 

\DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Looking Ahead}} \DIFaddend Overall, this landscape suggests a future rich with opportunity. \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{And there are already some practical use cases for which we provide tutorials at }\url{https://gpmbook.lamalab.org/tutorial-literature.html}\DIFadd{.
}\DIFaddend But realizing the potential impact of \glspl{gpm} demands clear-eyed caution: while it is now deceptively easy to spin up prototypes, transforming them into robust, reliable tools is a far more arduous task. \autocite{sculley2014machine}
More crucial still is our need for rigorous measurement and feedback---whether in the construction of evaluation suites, the calibration of reward functions for reinforcement learning, or the design of sensible governance. 
No single discipline can shoulder this alone; chemists, policy experts, and computer scientists must broaden their ranks and collaborate. 
This is particularly \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{true }\DIFaddend since science has always benefited from embracing a diversity of approaches. 
While \gls{gpm}-powered approaches for science, such as \enquote{AI scientists}, hold promise, a myopic focus on \enquote{AI scientists} might lead to \enquote{scientific monocultures}.\autocite{Savitsky_2025} 
We hope this review lowers the barrier to entry to the background and applications of \glspl{gpm} in the chemical sciences, inviting a wider spectrum of contributors to adopt a systems-science mindset---and, in doing so, to help harness the best of what \glspl{gpm} can offer for tackling the chemical sciences’ most persistent and pressing challenges.
